# GojoGo — Build Progress

See [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) for the full architecture and milestone plan. This file tracks what's actually done.

> **2026-08-05 — dinner, ordered at lunchtime. Phase 4 M5, scheduled orders and changing one before a kitchen sees it.** M4 predicted the cheap half of this — "a scheduled pickup is the same poller with no `pickupLead`" — and that turned out to be true and to be about a fifth of the milestone, because the timing was never the hard part. **The hard part is deciding when the kitchen finds out**, and everything that follows from an order existing for six hours before anybody is asked about it. Seven decisions it turns on. **The money is held at checkout, exactly as always** — the alternative, holding at promotion so a balance is not tied up all afternoon, fails at 19:10 for a 20:00 dinner, which is the one moment nothing can be done about it; money reserved early is the price of a promise. **No seventh status, for the fifth milestone running**: "scheduled" is not a stage of an order, because CONFIRMED already means "placed, waiting for the restaurant to answer" and that is precisely what a booking is — what differs is `queued_at`, a timestamp, and its null carries two facts that matter (no kitchen has seen this, so it is still the customer's to rewrite; and nobody has been asked anything, so the accept timeout has nothing to count). **The kitchen is told at promotion, not at placement**, which moved `OrderPlaced` out of checkout and into one `announce` — the event exists to put an order in front of a person, and a push at noon about an eight o'clock dinner is a push somebody will have forgotten by the time it matters. **A kitchen's prep estimate answers "how long do I need", not "when do I start"**: `readyAt = max(now + prep, the booking's target)`, or a kitchen accepting at six with a twenty-minute estimate has an eight o'clock dinner cooked by twenty past, which is not an early delivery but a cold one — and it is `max` rather than the target outright, so a kitchen that needs longer than there is slips the promise visibly instead of the system promising on their behalf. **A booking does not occupy the one-live-order slot** (booking Thursday's lunch must not stop somebody eating tonight), and the converse is accepted rather than deferred: a promotion can land while another order is in flight, so the active response grows `orders` and `scheduled` beside the flat `order` it has always had. **A change is the order placed again** — same body, same `PUT`, re-priced from the menu, with the hold moved by the difference and the revision in the idempotency key — and it is possible exactly while no kitchen has been told, which is "before preparation begins" read literally: a slice on a kitchen's screen is a question somebody is answering, and rewriting it under them is a different order they never agreed to. **A restaurant that has closed since is cancelled at promotion** rather than left to time out ten minutes later. One defect found and fixed in review, in this milestone's own new path: re-pricing a change resolves its promotion codes again, and the resolver counts this customer's redemptions — including *this order's own row* — so a once-per-customer code would have been refused on the very order carrying it; the redemptions are now released before the re-price, not after, with the ordering pinned by a test. `mvn test` **506/506** (25 new), `ModularityTests` green with no new module edge — the milestone is again entirely inside `delivery`. iOS `BUILD SUCCEEDED`. **Not deployed** — needs `V42`, no `cdk deploy`. *(Check the running image and `git fetch` before believing that: the same label has decayed nine times in this file.)*

> **2026-08-04 — you can now walk in and get it. Phase 4 M4, pickup / collect-in-store.** The gap that the vision, the plan and the storefront's own `info` block had all been assuming was already filled. **It is a fulfilment kind, not a delivery with a courier who happens to be nobody** — and that distinction is the milestone: the nobody-courier version needs a null courier in every screen, a zero fee on every receipt and a job dispatch must never be offered, each special-cased separately, while a kind is one branch taken at exactly three places (pricing, the courier search, who types the code). Four things it turns on. **The kind belongs to the order and not to the sub-order**, which is the one place this departs from what M3 predicted: per-merchant reads well until the money is followed, and then a mixed order has to say whether it is finished when the courier is done or when the customer eventually walks to the other counter — with one hold, one settlement and one terminal moment to answer with. So a checkout is one kind, and a cart whose kitchens do not all offer collection is refused at the kitchen that cannot, by name. **The code inverts, and that is one rule rather than two exceptions**: M2 had the kitchen show and the courier type because the courier is the one who cannot be paid until the step is done; on a collection the payee is behind the counter, so the customer shows and the kitchen types — which is what SPECS §5 asked for in the first place. Underneath both: *the code is displayed to the party that does not type it, and typed by the party who is paid for finishing the step.* **Readiness stops being only an estimate** — `ready_marked_at` sits beside `ready_at`, because for a collection the estimate is when to set off and the mark is "it is on the counter"; it drives `READY_FOR_PICKUP`, a push-only word that is **not a seventh status**, for the fourth milestone running. And **the money needed no new arithmetic**: no fee, no tip, and `settle` builds the same splits with the courier's two at zero, which the ledger drops because a zero fee is not an entry. `mvn test` **478/478**, iOS `BUILD SUCCEEDED`. Not deployed: `V41` and an image. Also corrected here: **M2 and M3 were deployed all along** — rev 55 on image `85627ca`, which is `HEAD` — the ninth time a label in this file has decayed, and the fastest yet, since the one claiming otherwise was written the same day.

> **2026-08-04 — one checkout, several kitchens. Phase 4 M3, multi-merchant orders.** The milestone M2 predicted the shape of: it said the pickup code would have to *move down* to the sub-order rather than be left alone, and that turned out to be the spine of the whole thing — **the typed digits are now what say which counter a courier is standing at**, so a bag cannot be marked collected from the wrong restaurant. Four decisions it turns on. **The parent keeps the six statuses and the one courier; the slice gets exactly one word of its own** (`COLLECTED`), because "this kitchen's bag is in hand" is a fact about one merchant and without it a collected slice and a cooking one on the same order are indistinguishable — no seventh *order* status, for the third milestone running. **The courier search waits for every kitchen and is timed to the slowest** (`max(readyAt) − pickupLead`, still `DispatchRequest.startAfter`), because a search opened on the fast kitchen's clock is a courier standing at a counter waiting for the slow one; a rejection counts as an answer, so one kitchen saying no releases the search rather than stalling it. **Cancelling one kitchen releases exactly that slice's share** — food − discount + its own delivery fee — with the service fee and the tip staying the order's, and `released_cents` making settlement's old invariant hold with a second term: **splits + released = held**, or nothing settles. And **the per-leg multi-courier split is deliberately not built**, which is made honest by a cap rather than left unstated: `delivery.order.max.merchants` = 3, because three counters is a trip one person makes and five is a relay. Wire compatibility is total — the legacy single-merchant checkout still places, every flat field on `OrderDto` stays populated, and the kitchen's queue DTO is unchanged in shape with its id now naming a sub-order. `mvn test` **447/447** (28 new), iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**. **Not deployed** — needs `V40`, no infra change. *(Check the running image before believing that: the same label has decayed eight times in this file.)*

> **2026-08-03 — the driver's half of the trip, found missing by the second prod E2E.** TestFlight surfaced four things in one session, and they share a root: **Phase 3 M3 built the ride's driver *API* and never built its driver *screen*.** (1) **Accepting a trip made it vanish** — the offer card cleared, the dispatch assignment sat in state nothing rendered, and the driver was BUSY with no way to see, run or finish the job. Driver Mode now runs the real trip: the ride behind a RIDE assignment is read from `travel`, the existing navigation map guides origin→pickup then pickup→dropoff (Mapbox on the phone, per the M3 seam), and the trip advances by the driver's own buttons — *arrived*, *rider aboard*, *complete*, with cancel until the rider is in the car. (2) **The offer card showed 0.0 km / no fare** because dispatch deliberately carries no money and the card had nothing else to read; a new driver-side read, `GET /v1/travel/rides/{id}/offered` — gated on the dispatch offer book exactly as `counter` is — now fills in the fare, the priced distance/duration and the rider's name (with a straight-line×1.3 estimate as first paint). The same read fixed a latent leak-shape in `toDto`: a viewer on the driver's side now sees only the negotiation addressed to them, never a rival's price. (3) **Negotiation existed on the server and the rider's screen but the driver had no way in** — the offer card gains *offer another price* (which also declines in dispatch, as the architecture always said it should), a card tracks the rider's answer, and a rider's counter can be taken into a confirmed trip. Counteroffers in either direction now push (`RideOfferMade`) — a 30-second offer nobody's phone announces is unanswerable. (4) **The push beat the screen**: the offer poll runs on 4s, APNs on ~1, so the phone rang before the card existed; the push handler now routes by payload `type` and refreshes the offer book / live ride immediately. `mvn test` **413/413** (2 new). Not deployed; no schema change.
>
> **Amended the same day — the second E2E found the two halves the first one couldn't reach.** (1) **A won negotiation reported itself as lost.** The negotiated path declines in dispatch and counters in travel — by design — so when the rider accepted the counter, `DispatchApi.assign` found the driver's offer row DECLINED and its "flip to ACCEPTED" was gated on the offer still being *open*. `currentAssignment` walks accepted offers, found nothing, and the winning driver's own app said "the rider went with somebody else" while the rider stood waiting for a car whose driver had just been told to go home. `assign` now closes the offer row ACCEPTED unconditionally: the row must record the acceptance that actually happened, however it arrived. (2) **The rider's completion screen erased itself.** `/rides/live` excludes COMPLETED — correctly; a finished trip is not "in a car" — but the app refreshed a *tracked* ride through it, so the "Trip complete" push cleared the rating screen at the exact moment it announced it. A tracked ride now refreshes by id; the completion also reloads the vehicle-verification invites (they're minted *at* completion, and the screen's one load at appear predates the trip), and a completion push tapped after a relaunch re-adopts the ride using the payload's `refId` — with `tokenCost` (a driver-only field) as the seat test. `mvn test` **414/414** (1 new).

> **2026-08-02 — My World is now GojoMessages, and it is a bigger thing than it was. Re-spec'd from a product doc and built the same day — M1 and M2 (see those sections); the phase is code-complete and undeployed.** The doc (`go2..pdf`) opens by retiring the name — *"not my world anymore"* — and then quietly changes the shape underneath it. What is live is a WhatsApp-style **chat** surface with a phone-verified identity attached. What is spec'd is a **private social network**: a graph of **phone numbers, where a username is never a way to find anybody**, **circles** as the audience primitive (custom groups, one person in several), and **its own posts, stories and carousels** published either to everyone who has your number or to named circles. The home screen becomes a feed — stories, then posts from people whose number you have, then the messages list. The chat detail screen is explicitly unchanged, which is worth honouring: it is the one part of this product that is finished. The public `social` feed stays where it is; the doc confirms it ("now on gojogo we are to have the main feed"), so this is a split rather than a replacement — and it turns the Phase 2c story-reply boundary from an anticipation into a rule. **Two things it did not settle, both now decided.** First, the doc reads like *one* phone-verified account (sign up with Apple / Google / phone; Apple and Google add a number later) — and the call is to **keep two identities**: a public app/social account with an `@handle`, and a separate phone-verified GojoMessages identity with its own name and photo. It is the WhatsApp/Instagram distinction, and it fits a network whose premise is "the people you actually know" — the face you show forty acquaintances is not the face you show the internet. So the doc's sign-up paragraph is *app* sign-up, GojoMessages keeps the first-entry setup it already has, and the username collected at sign-up is the public one, which is exactly why the private network needs none. It does mean the **rich profile** the doc describes (gender, city, languages, education, occupation, favourites) hangs off the **GojoMessages identity**, which today is a name and an avatar in a DynamoDB item — a real expansion with a storage question attached. Second, who owns GojoMessages content — **the half of that which mattered is now answered: a GojoMessages post is never public.** It is seen by the author's contacts or by the circles they picked and by nobody else, so the two content systems are genuinely two and the duplication is the point. It does **not** go in `social`: folding it in behind an audience column would make every feed, profile grid, search, archive and moderation read there responsible for a phone graph it cannot see, and one missed filter would publish a private post to the world. What is left is only *which* module owns it — extend `messaging`, which already holds the phone identity and the contacts, or a new module that depends on it for the graph. Either way **circles have to move server-side**; they are UI-only today and cannot be an audience while they exist only on a phone. Half of it is also **blocked on SNS SMS** (#3 below). Written up as **ARCHITECTURE §2b decision 4 + Phase 2f**; where it sits against Phase 4 M1 is a product call, not a technical one.
>
> **Amended the same day — names survive, and you can privately rename anyone.** "Phone numbers only" is a rule about **lookup**, not about names: you *add* somebody by number and only by number (the `@handle` lookup still dies), but a contact keeps showing under their **World `displayName`**, which is what the thread already renders. The first write of this said "no usernames in it at all" and that was too strong — it killed the public `@handle` as a way to *find* someone, which is right, and took the person's name with it, which was not. On top of that, GojoMessages gains a **per-viewer contact alias**: rename anyone to whatever you like, see it everywhere their name appears, and it is invisible to them and to everybody else — a property of the edge from you to them, stored account-level in `messaging` (not device-local in `WorldPreference`; a rename is about the person, not the handset) and resolved per-caller at read time as *alias → World name → social name*. **The one thing that makes it a real slice rather than a lookup tweak:** two name stamps happen at *write* time and cannot carry a per-viewer value — `MessagingService.buildReply` stores `authorName` into the message row, and `notifyRecipients` resolves one `worldName(...)` for the whole recipient list before fan-out. Both need moving to read time / inside the per-recipient loop, or the alias will be right in the thread and wrong in the quoted reply above it.

> **2026-08-02 — the two taps get a witness. Phase 4 M2, handoff integrity + money.** M1 made the courier a real person and left the two moments that matter most **unproven**. A courier taps *I have the food* and the order moves; a courier taps *I handed it over* and the money moves. Nobody checked either one, and the second pays somebody. This milestone is the evidence behind those taps: a **pickup code** the kitchen displays and the courier types, a **delivery PIN** the customer reads out, a **contactless proof photo** for the doors nobody answers, the **courier↔customer thread**, and — the money half — the first way a driver or a courier can get their earnings *out* of the platform at all. Four decisions it turns on. **The code direction is reversed from SPECS §5**: the spec has the courier showing a code and the merchant confirming it, and this does the opposite, because the party who must act should be the party with the incentive — a busy merchant who forgets to tap strands a courier holding food they cannot mark collected, whereas a courier cannot be paid until they finish the step and can only learn the code by standing at that counter, which is the entire fact the code certifies. **A wrong code is a 200, not an error** — a courier who typed a 7 for a 1 has not made a bad request, and an error banner is the wrong thing to put in front of somebody holding a bag of food; it is also the only shape in which the attempt counter *commits* instead of being rolled back with the exception that reported it. **The counting is deliberately asymmetric** — never at the counter, where the merchant is standing right there and a lockout would strand somebody holding food, always at the door, where the risk runs the other way — and hitting the limit opens a photo fallback rather than a dead end. That fallback is a hole, it is documented as one, and the alternative (a courier in the street with a dinner nobody will accept because the customer is asleep) is worse. And **the worker payout surface belongs to `dispatch`**, not to a vertical, which surfaced something the merchant case had been hiding: a merchant is paid into a `MERCHANT` account that has no way to receive a top-up, but a driver or courier is paid into their own **`USER AVAILABLE`** bucket — the same pocket a card top-up lands in — so a payout over a raw balance would be this platform's first **card→bank path**. It is capped at what the person actually earned. `mvn test` **399/399** (58 new), iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**. **Not deployed** — needs `V38`, no infra change. *(Check the running image before believing that: the same label on M1 was wrong within the day.)*
>
> **2026-08-02 — the courier stops being four hardcoded names. Phase 4 M1, real couriers.** The swap 2b M4 explicitly designed, cashed in: `OrderFulfilmentJob` walked every delivery along a *simulated timeline* — a kitchen that always took twenty seconds, a courier drawn from a roster of four — and the promise was that the state machine, the events and the API were the real thing so dispatch would replace only the job. It did. **The six order statuses, both domain events and every customer-facing endpoint are unchanged**, `dispatch` did not change by a single line, and `delivery` hung its assignment off the same two events `travel` uses — which is precisely what `travel`'s listener predicted in a comment when it was written. Three decisions the milestone turns on. **The kitchen had to become a person before the courier could**: deleting the job removes the only thing that ever moved an order, so a restaurant now has a queue, accepts with a prep estimate and can turn one down — that estimate is the first honest `readyAt` this vertical has ever had, and it is what schedules the courier search at `readyAt − pickupLead`, which is `DispatchRequest.startAfter`, the field M1 built for exactly this and no caller had ever used. **The state machine did not grow a word**: "the food is cooking and nobody has taken the delivery" is a `courier_search` column beside the status, not a seventh one, because it is a problem with an order rather than a stage of it — and a new status is a word every client in the field has to learn. And **an exhausted search is flagged, never retried and never auto-cancelled**: dispatch's job row is unique per (kind, ref) by design, re-asking is the same question to the same empty city, and throwing away food that may already be made is a decision for the people involved. The courier is finally *paid* — the delivery fee and the tip that 2e M3 parked with the platform under their own ledger kinds, so it really was one payee and not an archaeology project. `mvn test` **320/320**, iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**. ~~**Not deployed** — needs `V35`, no infra change.~~ **Deployed** — confirmed against the live account 2026-08-02: task definition **rev 45**, image `e333f24`, `V35` applied and prod is at schema **v37**. That label was stale, which is the **eighth** recorded decay in this file and the second one it caused itself.

> **2026-08-02 — the trip gets a witness. Phase 3 M5, community verification + the safety pack.** The milestone that closes Phase 3's core, and the one where the $30 stake finally does the job it was collected for: **passengers check the car**, and are paid to, out of the driver's own locked money. A reviewer looked at a photograph of a registration document; a passenger looked at the actual car with the actual driver in it, which is the only check that catches a swapped plate. Alongside it, the two items 2e M5 deferred here because both describe a trip: **live share links and SOS**, plus the **emergency contacts** they reach. Three decisions the milestone turns on. **Share links are their own platform module** — `share` holds a *pointer* and renders the payload on every read through a `ShareableResource` SPI the vertical implements, because a link showing a stored position is a screenshot rather than a tracking link (the fourth instance of the platform-defines / vertical-implements shape). **The vehicle became a `VehicleRef`** pushed down to dispatch — its id is what makes a passenger's answer land on the car they actually rode in, and its `verified` flag is what carries the badge to a kerb. And **SOS never dials**: the server flags the trip, mints the link, pushes to the contacts who are GojoGo accounts, and hands the local emergency number back for the *phone* to call — a backend placing an emergency call is a claim about integration with emergency services this system has not earned. `mvn test` **293/293**, iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**. **Deployed the same day** as task definition **rev 37** / image `9fc9394`; `V33` applied and prod is at schema **v33**. Worth one note on the doc rather than the code: this milestone's own "NOT deployed" label was **stale within the hour** — CI rolled it off the push while the label was still being written, which is the *seventh* recorded decay in this file and the first one this file caused itself.

> **2026-08-01 — the ride loop, run against production for the first time.** An **approved driver and an approved restaurant now exist in prod**, and with them the first end-to-end exercise of everything Phase 3 has built. It found a **defect that shipped in M3 and had been live ever since**: `RideService.confirmFromDispatch` runs from an `AFTER_COMMIT` listener, where the just-committed transaction's resources are still bound to the thread, so plain `@Transactional` joined a transaction that was already finished and the first flush threw *"no transaction is in progress"*. The damage was worse than a 500 — dispatch had already assigned the job, so **the driver was marked busy for a ride that never confirmed and the rider was told nothing** until the request TTL expired five minutes later. The negotiated path never hit it, because that one confirms inside the rider's own request, which is why M3's tests and its deploy both came up green. Fixed with `REQUIRES_NEW` on both listener entry points plus a structural test, and redeployed. **This is the clearest case yet for the thing this repo keeps rediscovering: a path nothing has ever executed is not a working path**, and 212 green unit tests said nothing about it.

> **2026-08-01 — rides start earning. Phase 3 M4, ride-hailing tokens.** M3 shipped a ride that settles as a single rider→driver transfer with **no commission**, which means the transport vertical has so far earned the platform exactly nothing. That is the vision's model, not an oversight: the driver keeps the whole fare and pays a **token** to accept the job. So this milestone builds the token — packs bought out of the wallet, a per-category-per-distance price list, a debit at CONFIRMED, a refund on a cancellation, a low-balance push, and a driver-facing screen. The decision the milestone turns on: **dispatch must not offer a trip to a driver who cannot pay for it, and dispatch must not learn what a token is** — so it gained a third platform→vertical SPI, `WorkerEligibility`, which `travel` implements. `mvn test` **249/249**, iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**. **Not deployed** — needs `V32`, no infra change.

> **2026-08-01 — rides, deployed. Phase 3 M3, the ride loop.** The `travel` vertical is built: a quote for every category, a request that actually asks dispatch, **fare negotiation** with counteroffers as rows, the trip, one payment at completion and mutual blind ratings. This is the first thing in the system that ever gives dispatch a job — M1 built the matching and M2 built the drivers, and neither had been asked for anything. The decision the milestone turns on: **dispatch's offer book asks "will you do this job" and has no opinion about money**, so a driver who wants a different fare declines there and counters here, and when the rider accepts, `travel` calls the new `DispatchApi.assign` — with the job row still the arbiter, so a counter taken a second too late loses exactly as a second driver would. `mvn test` **212/212**, iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**. **Deployed** as task definition **rev 29** / image `19a1107`; `V31` applied cleanly and prod is at schema **v31**.

> **2026-08-01 — deployed, and a stale "not deployed" caught.** **Phase 3 M1 + M2 are live**: image `b3901f6`, task definition **rev 27**, `V29` and `V30` applied cleanly at startup (health 200 through the ALB is a real database round-trip, and the Flyway log names both). No `cdk deploy` — neither milestone adds a secret, an env var or an IAM action. **The deploy also found that 2e M5 and tagging+replies had been live since before this session**: the service was already running `432ecae` at rev 26, prod was at schema **v28**, and the task role already carried the three Cognito admin actions — so both the image roll *and* the infra deploy M5 needed had happened. Every "NOT deployed" label on those two was **stale**, which is the third time this file has decayed that way (see the 2026-07-30 deploy audit and the 2026-07-31 "Needs YOU" audit). The pattern is always the same: **nothing fails when a thing is done and the doc isn't updated.**

> **2026-08-01 — the driver pipeline, and the app stops pretending.** **Phase 3 M2** is built on top of M1: **vehicles** (papers, expiry dates, one active per driver, plate unique per *region*), the **$30 stake** as a real ledger movement into the driver's own STAKING bucket, the **KYC fee charged out of it**, provisioning that names the **active vehicle's category**, and a sweep that flags a lapsed registration and takes its driver off the road. On the app side three steps stopped being theatre — a stake page that slept and charged nothing, a submit that inserted a role into a local `Set`, and two tiles you tapped to *claim* you'd photographed your papers — and **Driver Mode is now live against the real registry** for anybody who has one, with the demo kept for everybody else. Submitting no longer makes you a driver: it queues you, and the screen says so. `mvn test` **183/183**, iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**. **Not deployed** — needs `V30`, no infra change.

> **2026-08-01 — somewhere for a driver to land.** **Phase 2e is closed** and **Phase 3 M1 (the platform `dispatch` module)** is built: a registry, presence, ring-by-ring candidate search, an offer book, and the `DriverProvisioningApi`/`CourierProvisioningApi` that a `DRIVER` or `COURIER` approval has been **409ing toward since 2b M6**. Two deviations from SPECS §3, both stated rather than discovered: **presence is Postgres and not Redis** (a bounding box in SQL, the real distance measured in Java — there are zero workers and an ElastiCache cluster bills by the hour), and **positions arrive over REST and not the WebSocket** (the existing socket has no inbound route; building one belongs in M3, where a position is actually *watched*). `mvn test` **156/156**, no iOS by the plan — Driver Mode is M2. **Not deployed** — needs `V29` applied and an image rolled, behind 2e M5 which also needs `cdk deploy`.

> **2026-07-31 — the launch blockers.** Phase 2e **M5 (trust & safety baseline)** is built and closes Phase 2e: **blocking**, a platform **`moderation`** module with a human queue, and **account deletion**. These are not features — App Store guideline 1.2 (report + block + takedown) and 5.1.1(v) (delete your account in the app) make them conditions of shipping user-generated content at all, and the app had none of them. Along the way it corrected two boundaries that had quietly gone wrong: `auth` now holds the *only* definition of "is this caller a platform operator" (`partner` had a private copy), and `POST /v1/auth/session` moved into `profile` — same URL, no client change — because `auth → profile` became half a cycle the moment other modules started depending on `auth`. **Not built and moved to Phase 3 M5: SOS and public share links**, both of which describe a trip that does not exist yet. `mvn test` **112/112**, iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**. **Not deployed** — needs `V28` applied and an image rolled.

> **2026-07-31 — money moves.** Phase 2e **M3 (payments + the GoJo Wallet)** is built, deployed and prod-verified: one double-entry ledger, Stripe only at the boundary, and a delivery order that is finally *paid for* — held at checkout, split at delivery between merchant, courier and platform, released on a cancel. Also new: the platform config registry (SPECS §14), promotions and tips, the `OrderStatusChanged` push consumer, an iOS wallet + settings page, and a **`schema-check` CI workflow** that migrates a real Postgres and boots the app — added because three of this milestone's four deploy attempts failed on things nothing local could catch. Next paid milestone per the plan: **Phase 2e M4 (storefront config)**; Phase 3's stake and tokens now have buckets to live in.

> **2026-07-30 — plan re-baselined, nothing built:** Phases 3+ were re-planned against the full GoJoGo product vision (five pillars). New in ARCHITECTURE.md: §2b pillar→module map + two decisions (a business **is** a `profile`; the GoJo Wallet — staking/tokens/rewards — is a ledger inside `payments`), a new **Phase 2e** (business profiles, real admin principal + the missing partner admin-create, wallet, storefront JSON) that should run **before** dispatch, re-scoped Phases 3–7, and §10b, the **GoJoAdmin integration contract** (GoJoAdmin is a separate app that will consume existing owner-JWT `/mine` surfaces + `/v1/partner/admin/**`; no parallel data path). **[SPECS.md](SPECS.md) (new)** fills the missing logic gap-by-gap against the vision text: money flows + capture/settlement per transaction type, ride negotiation + dispatch matching state machines, driver staking/tokens/community-verification mechanics, multi-merchant sub-orders + handoff codes + disputes, promotions/reviews/inventory, digital + ownership-transfer flows, services booking rules, storefront JSON block contract, a trust & safety baseline (report/block/moderation/account deletion — a launch blocker the vision never names, added as **Phase 2e M5**), share/tracking links, phone signup + MFA, notification coverage matrix, and a platform config registry; §15 lists what's deliberately deferred (cash, errands, GoJoAds, multi-currency…). Next paid milestone per that plan: **Phase 2e M1 (business profiles)** — **built, deployed and prod-verified the same day** (see the milestone list); **Phase 2e M2 (real admin principal + admin-side create)** is next. (The 2b M5/M6 + 2c "deploy backlog" turned out to already be live — CI had rolled it; see the 2026-07-30 deploy-audit note in the incidents log.)

## ⚠️ Needs YOU (things Claude can't do — external accounts, devices, money, policy)

These are the only open items that require you personally; everything else in Phase 1–2 is built + deployed + verified.

1. **APNs device test** — ~~enable the Push Notifications capability~~ **the capability is on** (`aps-environment` is in [GojoGo.entitlements](GojoGo/GojoGo.entitlements), which the app couldn't build with otherwise). What's left is the actual proof: run the app on a **physical iPhone** and confirm a real push arrives. **There is a live mismatch to fix first:** the entitlement says `development` (a plain Xcode build mints a **sandbox** token) while the deployed backend runs `APNS_PRODUCTION=true`, and Apple rejects a sandbox token on the production endpoint with `BadDeviceToken`. For a dev-device test, deploy with `APNS_PRODUCTION=false`; flip it back for TestFlight/App Store builds, whose entitlement is `production`. The flag lives in [fargate-stack.ts](infra/lib/fargate-stack.ts) (**not** app-stack — App Runner is retired).
2. **CloudFront** — **still open** (re-checked 2026-07-31: zero distributions, `ENABLE_CLOUDFRONT = false`). The AWS account is unverified for CloudFront; only AWS Support can lift it. Until then media is public-read from S3. (Flip `ENABLE_CLOUDFRONT` in media-stack when verified.)
3. **Real SMS OTP** — **still open, and the bypass was deliberately turned back on 2026-08-04.** Sequence worth knowing, because this item decayed in *both* directions: at some point real SMS was switched on, which made the dev code inert on its own (`WorldProperties.hasDevCode` honours it only while `smsEnabled` is false) — a verify with `424242` returned 401 and `/phone/start` really did publish to SNS. But SNS SMS is still sandboxed, so only pre-verified numbers can receive anything, and a second test account could not be verified at all. The bypass was restored on purpose to unblock the E2EE Phase C two-party test: one CDK context flag, `-c worldDevOtpCode=424242`, which sets the code **and** `WORLD_SMS_ENABLED=false` together so the two can never disagree. It is carried in `scripts/deploy-backend.sh`'s context block and `--infra` prints a three-line warning on every deploy. **So the original wording is true again: anyone who knows that code can pass phone verification in production.** Empty `WORLD_DEV_OTP_CODE` in that script the day SNS SMS leaves the sandbox; nothing else needs to change. SNS SMS is in the account sandbox (only verified numbers). Get SNS SMS production access (AWS Support) + a sender id, or swap to Twilio/Vonage Verify. Then **clear `WORLD_OTP_DEV_CODE`** before launch — this is a launch blocker, not a nicety. **Upgraded 2026-08-02 from "a launch blocker" to "a roadmap blocker":** the GojoMessages re-spec makes a phone number the *only* way to add somebody and a first-class way to sign up, so this now gates a whole product area rather than one verification step. Nothing in Phase 2f that touches the graph can be more than a plan until it clears.

6. **Gojo Admin owns restaurant creation** (decided 2026-07-27) — the in-app apply flow is gone. Until Gojo Admin exists, the only way to create a restaurant is the review surface by hand — which **now has an admin-side create** (`POST /v1/partner/admin/applications`, shipped in 2e M2, so the "no create endpoint" note here was stale), authenticated by your own JWT once you're in the `platform-admin` group (#5) or by the break-glass token. **You are the KYC reviewer** either way: approvals are a human decision and there is no admin role in this app. After the M6 deploy, read the generated token once — `aws secretsmanager get-secret-value --secret-id gojogo/partner-admin-token --query SecretString --output text` — and keep it somewhere safe; nothing else can approve a restaurant, which means nothing else can put one in GojoDelivery. Runbook and the exact curls are in the Phase 2b M6 section. ~~Worth deciding before launch: whether a real reviewer is you, or whether this becomes a small internal web tool.~~ **There is now a local one** — [`tools/admin-console`](tools/admin-console/README.md), a stopgap that renders the same `/v1/partner/admin/**`, `/v1/moderation/admin/**` and (since 3 M5) `/v1/travel/admin/sos` surface rather than curl. It runs on your laptop, holds no data, keeps the break-glass token server-side so it never reaches the browser, and needs no CORS change in prod because it proxies. It does not answer the *staffing* question — reports and applications still pile up until a person opens it — but working the queue is no longer a shell session.
7. ~~**Turn Sumsub on in AWS**~~ — **DONE 2026-07-30.** Secret created, production credentials in place, `cdk deploy` applied (rev 16), startup log confirms `production environment … webhooks verified`. What remains is yours: **press "Test webhook"** in the Sumsub cockpit for the first real signed delivery, and **rotate both credentials** — the production app token and secret key were pasted through a chat session, so treat them as exposed (regenerate in Dev Space → App Tokens, then `put-secret-value` on `gojogo/sumsub` and force a new deployment). Original steps kept below for the production-key rotation and for whoever sets this up next:
   - ~~**Create the secret.**~~ **Done 2026-07-30** — `gojogo/sumsub` exists, holding all three keys (`appToken` / `secretKey` / `webhookSecret`), verified readable and parsing. ARN: `arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-east-1:578109959809:secret:gojogo/sumsub-gHmmld`. The credentials are **production** (`prd:` token — swapped in the same day, once the webhook turned out to have been created in Production mode) and live only in Secrets Manager: Sumsub shows a secret key once and never again, so this secret is the only copy. Both halves were confirmed against `api.sumsub.com` before being written — a production mint returns an `_act-jwt-…` token where sandbox returns `_act-sbx-jwt-…`, which is the cheapest way to tell the two apart (the API host is identical). **Verification levels are shared across environments**, so `id-and-liveness` needed no production-side setup; app tokens and webhooks are the only environment-specific pieces. Two consequences worth stating plainly: checks are now **billed** and involve **real people's documents**, and because sandbox has no approval algorithms the verdict path has never been exercised end to end — the first real applicant is the first full run. Deploy with its ARN: `cdk deploy GojoGoFargateStack -c sumsubSecretArn=arn:… -c imageTag=<current>` (plus the usual `-c domainName/-c certificateArn`). **Production is the same secret with a non-`sbx:` token** — no code change. The startup log says which environment it came up in; check it, because sandbox verdicts are simulated and the API host is identical.
   - **Create the webhook FIRST, then the secret** — Sumsub generates the webhook secret when you create the webhook, and the endpoint returns 503 until that value is deployed, so doing it the other way round means two deploys and a failed "Test webhook". Cockpit → **Dev Space → Webhooks → Webhook Manager → Create webhook**, with the Sandbox/Production toggle (upper right) set to match your token: receiver **HTTP address**, target `https://api.gojogo.app/v1/kyc/webhook`, type **`applicantReviewed`** (add `applicantPending` / `applicantOnHold` / `applicantReset` for the intermediate states), applicant type **Individual**, algorithm **SHA256**, and leave *Resend failed webhooks* **on** — the handler is idempotent on the delivery id precisely so retries are safe. Copy the generated secret into `webhookSecret` in the command above, deploy, *then* press **Test webhook** and expect a 200. Sandbox and production webhooks are separate configurations; going live means creating it again in Production mode.
   - **Pick the level.** `id-and-liveness` is the default; your account also has `id-only` and `idv-and-phone-verification`. Change with `-c sumsubLevelName=…`, no code change. There is **no company/KYB level** in the account — business papers stay on the existing private-upload + human review path, which is why they didn't drop out of `requiredDocuments`.
   - **One workflow consequence worth knowing:** the admin-side create (`POST /v1/partner/admin/applications`) can still be filed for a merchant, but **submitting it now waits on that owner passing their own ID check in the app**. Nobody can do a liveness check on someone else's behalf. The refusal names the status so it's obvious what's missing.

13. **The Sumsub verdict path has still never run (new, 2026-08-01)** — and the E2E that approved a driver and a restaurant did **not** change that. Both applications gate submission on a verified identity, and rather than put a fabricated identity through a live, billed KYC/AML vendor, Sumsub was deliberately deployed **unconfigured** for ~15 minutes (rev 33) so the app's own document-review fallback carried the approvals; the production secret was never read or rewritten, and rev 34 restored it. So what is proven is the *fallback*, the review queue and the approval → provisioning chain. What is still unproven is everything downstream of a real applicant: the MobileSDK token mint, the webhook verdict, and the retryable/final status split the app branches on. **The first real driver or merchant is still the first full run**, and it will cost a real check against a real person's documents. Worth deciding: whether you want to spend one sandbox cycle proving it first (a sandbox app token in a *second* secret, deployed with `-c sumsubSecretArn=<sandbox>`, which never touches the production one), or accept that the first live applicant is the test.

Rare/edge (decide if you care): account-linking is one-directional (Google-first → later email self-signup on the same address fails) and orphaned media uploads are never cleaned up — **both deployed 2026-07-23, pending behavioral E2E** (see Known issues): a clearer `PreSignUp_SignUp` error, and a report-only orphan sweep with reference tracking.

- **IAM user:** `gojogo-builder`, policy `GojoGoMilestone1Policy` at **v6** ([iam-policy-milestone1.json](iam-policy-milestone1.json) tracks it: v3–v4 added CDK bootstrap/lookup + App Runner SLR perms; v5–v6 added Secrets Manager on `gojogo/*`, CloudWatch Logs read on `/aws/apprunner/*`, VPC-creation perms from the reverted private-networking attempt)

## Milestone status

- [x] **Phase 4 · Milestone 5 — Scheduled orders + changing one before a kitchen sees it** ✅ **built 2026-08-05** — **NOT deployed**: needs `V42` applied and an image rolled; no `cdk deploy` (no new secret, env var or IAM action). *(`V41` from M4 is still outstanding too, so a single roll carries both.)*

  **The slice M4 left behind, and the one it thought was cheap.** M4's own note said a scheduled pickup is "the same poller with no `pickupLead`", which is true and is roughly a fifth of this. The timing was never the difficulty. **The difficulty is that an order can now exist for six hours before anybody is asked about it**, and almost everything below is a consequence of that one fact.

  **The money is held at checkout, exactly as it always has been.** The tempting alternative is to hold at promotion, so a customer's balance is not tied up all afternoon — and its failure mode is a hold that fails at 19:10 for a 20:00 dinner, which is the precise moment nothing can be done about it. Money reserved early is what a promise costs; a promise that can quietly fail is not one. So `place` did not change its payment path at all.

  **No seventh status, for the fifth milestone running.** "Scheduled" is not a stage of an order: CONFIRMED already means *placed, waiting for the restaurant to answer*, and that is exactly what a booking is. What differs is whether the restaurant has been asked, which is `queued_at` — a timestamp — and whose null carries two load-bearing facts at once: no kitchen has seen this order, so it is still the customer's to rewrite; and nobody has been asked anything, so the accept timeout has nothing to count. That second one is not defensive. `unansweredIds` measured from `placedAt` would have cancelled tomorrow's lunch ten minutes after somebody ordered it, and a restaurant cannot fail to answer a question it has not been asked.

  **The kitchen is told at promotion, not at placement**, and that moved `OrderPlaced` out of checkout. Both paths now go through one `announce`, so there is a single place that decides what a kitchen is told and an ordinary order and a promoted one cannot drift apart. The reasoning is what the event is *for*: it exists to put an order in front of a person, and a push at noon about an eight o'clock dinner is a push somebody will have forgotten by the time it matters. `OrderPlaced.placedAt` still means when the customer ordered, which is now a different thing from when it arrived — said out loud on the record itself, because a consumer reading it as "how long has this been in front of me" would be wrong for exactly the orders this milestone adds.

  **A kitchen's prep estimate answers "how long do I need", not "when do I start".** `readyAt = max(now + prep, the booking's target)` — the target being the promised moment for a collection and that moment minus the dropoff for a delivery. Without the anchor a kitchen accepting at 18:15 with a twenty-minute estimate has an eight o'clock dinner cooked by 18:35, which is not an early delivery but a cold one. And it is **`max` rather than the target outright**, which is the honest half: a kitchen that needs longer than there is says so, `readyAt` slips past the promise and the customer's countdown moves with it. Clamping would be the system promising on the kitchen's behalf.

  **Promotion time is stored at placement, not derived on every poll.** `queue_at = scheduled_for − the slowest kitchen's own advertised time − the window they get to answer in`. Stored because a merchant editing their ETA must not move an order that has already been promised to somebody, and because the poller's query is then a partial index scan over the few rows that are actually waiting. The same arithmetic is what refuses a time nobody could cook for — and the refusal is stated as *an amount of notice* rather than a time of day, because the server has no idea what timezone the customer is standing in and "earliest 19:05" is a wrong sentence in most of the world. The exact instant rides on the quote, as `earliestScheduleAt`, so the picker's bounds are this basket's own rather than a constant the client guessed.

  **A booking does not occupy the one-live-order slot, and the converse is accepted rather than deferred.** Booking Thursday's lunch must not stop somebody eating tonight, so the checkout conflict check asks about orders being *fulfilled*. That means a promotion can land while another order is in flight — and holding food back over a rule about screens would be the worse answer — so `ActiveOrderResponse` grows `orders` (every live one, soonest first) and `scheduled` (bookings nobody has been told about) beside the flat `order`, which keeps carrying the soonest-arriving one. In the overwhelmingly common case that is the only one there is, so a client that predates the lists renders exactly what it always did.

  **A change is the order placed again.** `PUT /v1/delivery/orders/{id}` takes the same body `POST` does: baskets re-priced from the menu (never from the request, exactly as at checkout), promotions resolved again — a code that has since expired is refused here rather than silently kept — the address, note, handoff mode and fulfilment kind all replaced rather than patched, and the hold moved by the *difference* with the revision in the idempotency key. A difference rather than a release and a re-hold, because those two would appear on a statement as a refund and a second charge for an order that never stopped existing, and a failure between them would leave a live order with an empty escrow. The window is exactly "no kitchen has been told", which is the vision's "before preparation begins" read literally: a slice on a kitchen's screen is a question somebody is answering, and rewriting what is in it under them is not an edit but a different order they never agreed to. An ordinary order is in front of a kitchen from the moment it is placed and therefore has no editable window at all — its lever is cancelling, which it already had.

  **One defect found in review, in this milestone's own new path, and it is worth recording because it is the shape of mistake a re-price invites.** Resolving a promotion code counts what this customer has already redeemed — and this order's own redemption row is among them. Releasing the row *after* the re-price would therefore have refused a once-per-customer code on the very order that was carrying it, telling somebody they had already used a code they were holding. The redemptions are released **before** the re-price and re-recorded on the way out; a pricing failure rolls the whole thing back. The ordering is pinned by an `InOrder` test rather than left to a comment.

  **A restaurant that has closed since is the one thing about a booking that can have changed**, so promotion checks it: that slice is cancelled and refunded there and then rather than waiting ten minutes to fail an accept timeout, and if every kitchen has closed the order ends in one movement. Promotion is idempotent on the stamp, so two instances polling at once — or a redelivered promotion — tells nobody twice.

  **The poller is its own class, deliberately.** `OrderTimeoutJob` decides that *nobody did anything*, which is the last thing a clock is entitled to conclude in this vertical and the whole of what survived the simulated fulfilment job. `ScheduledOrderJob` carries out an instruction a person gave. Folding them together would produce one class whose job is "things that happen on a timer", which is a category rather than a responsibility. Nothing else was needed: the courier search still schedules itself off `DispatchRequest.startAfter` exactly as it has since M1, and dispatch did not change by a line — the fourth milestone running.

  **`V42` is four columns, one backfill and a partial index.** `queued_at` is backfilled to `placed_at` rather than defaulted, because the column has to be nullable for the orders this milestone introduces and a default would put a lie on those. Every existing order was in front of its kitchen from the moment it was placed, which is what the backfill says.

  **iOS**: a Now/Later pair in checkout under the delivery/collect pair — the rarer decision, and the one people arrive at second — with the wheel appearing only once "Later" is chosen and bounded by the server's own window, so a time that would be refused cannot be offered. The place button becomes "Book it". A **Booked for later** section on the delivery home, above the rails because it is the only thing on that screen the reader has already committed money to, as a list rather than a rail: a row somebody has to scroll sideways to find is a row they will not cancel in time. Tapping one opens a sheet with the time and a cancel — deliberately *not* a second checkout, since the server would take a whole new basket but the two things anybody opens it for are the time and their money back. The kitchen's queue card carries "Booked for Thu 7 Aug at 8:00 PM", which is the one thing a kitchen has to be told about a booking, since the prep picker underneath it answers a different question.

  **Verified:** `mvn test` **506/506** on Corretto 21 (25 new), `ModularityTests` green — no new module edge, and the milestone is again entirely inside `delivery`. The new tests are led by the cases only a booking can have: a kitchen that must not be told yet, a time this basket's slowest kitchen cannot make, a booking that must not block tonight's dinner, a prep estimate that must not pull the food forward, a kitchen that needs longer than there is, a restaurant that closed in the meantime, a promotion released before it is resolved again, and an order a kitchen already has refusing to be rewritten. iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**.

  **What is deliberately not built:** **turning a booking into "order it now"** (cancel and reorder — the money is released and re-held either way, and an inline promotion is a second path through the one piece of code that must not have two); **recurring orders**; **per-slice rescheduling** (the kind is the order's and so is the time, for the same reason M4 gave — one hold, one settlement, one terminal moment); and **opening hours**, which this vertical still does not model at all: a booking for three in the morning is accepted and the kitchen simply never answers it, which times out and refunds. That is the honest current behaviour rather than a silent one, but it is a hole and it is the obvious next thing here. Also still not built, and still Phase 4's own: **disputes**, a **courier rating** (fifth milestone running), **order-for-someone-else**, **category expansion**, and a **share link for a delivery**.

  **Unexercised.** Nothing here has run against the deployed backend. In order: a **booking placed and promoted** (does the kitchen's push arrive at the right time rather than at checkout, and does the queue row appear only then), a **change** (does the hold move by the difference and only the difference), and a **booking whose restaurant closes before promotion** — that last one because it is the only path where the server cancels something the customer never touched.

- [x] **Phase 4 · Milestone 4 — Pickup / collect-in-store** ✅ **built 2026-08-04** — **NOT deployed**: needs `V41` applied and an image rolled; no `cdk deploy` (no new secret, env var or IAM action). *(Checked before writing it: the running task definition is rev 55 on image `85627ca`, which is `HEAD`, and health is 200 — so `V38` and `V40` **are** live and this is genuinely the only undeployed migration.)*

  **The gap that every part of the plan assumed was already filled.** Found on 2026-07-31: the vision, ARCHITECTURE §10 and the storefront `info` block (SPECS §9 — "hours/delivery/**pickup**") all take collection for granted, and nothing anywhere defined it. SPECS §5 calls it the cheapest milestone in Phase 4 and the one that unblocks the rest, and two milestones had already made it cheaper: M2 built the handoff code that a collection *is*, and M3 moved that code down to the sub-order, which is where a per-merchant counter wants it.

  **It is a fulfilment *kind*, not a delivery with a courier who happens to be nobody.** That distinction is the whole milestone. The nobody-shaped-courier version needs a null courier in every screen, a zero fee on every receipt, a job dispatch must never offer, and a special case at each of them — and every one of those has to be written anyway. A kind is one branch, taken at exactly three places: pricing, the courier search, and who types the code. `maybeOpenCourierSearch` returns on one line, `settle` did not change at all, and `OrderTimeoutJob` covers a silent kitchen for free.

  **The one place this departs from what M3 predicted: the kind belongs to the order, not the sub-order.** The resume note said the per-merchant `fulfilment_kind` wants to live on the slice, and that reads well until the money is followed. A mixed order needs a courier for some bags and not others, a delivery fee on some slices and not others, and then has to decide whether the parent is finished when the courier is done or when the customer eventually walks to the other counter — with **one hold, one settlement and one terminal moment** to answer that with. So a checkout is one kind, a cart whose kitchens do not all offer collection is refused out loud at the merchant that cannot (naming it), and somebody who wants both places two orders. What M3's per-slice code still buys is real: a customer collecting from two kitchens proves each counter separately, exactly as a courier does.

  **The code inverts, and that is one rule rather than two exceptions.** M2 reversed SPECS §5 for a delivery — the kitchen shows, the courier types — because the courier is the one who cannot be paid until the step is done. On a collection the payee is standing *behind* the counter (collection is what settles the order), so the same reasoning lands where §5 asked in the first place: **the customer shows, the kitchen types**. Underneath both: *the code is displayed to the party that does not type it, and typed by the party who is paid for completing the step.* Concretely `MerchantOrderDto.pickupCode` is **blank** on a pickup slice and `SubOrderDto.collectionCode` is populated — a kitchen that could read its own code could hand the food to nobody and settle the order.

  **A wrong code is a 200 that says so, and nothing is counted.** Same shape as both M2 verbs, same two reasons: a mistyped digit at a counter is not a bad request, and a thrown exception rolls back the very thing it was recording. `attemptsLeft` is always `-1` here — M2's asymmetry applied at the same kind of counter it was written for. Locking a kitchen out of handing over food they have already made is by far the worse failure, and the customer is standing right there.

  **Readiness stops being only an estimate.** `sub_order.ready_marked_at` sits *beside* `ready_at` rather than overwriting it, because for a collection the two are different facts told to a different person: the estimate is when to set off, and this is "it is on the counter". It drives **`READY_FOR_PICKUP`**, a push-only word that is not one of the six — the same shape M3 gave `SUB_CANCELLED`: the order has not moved, `notifications` turns it into "your order is ready to collect", and a client that has never heard it drops it in the default arm. **No seventh status, for the fourth milestone running**; a pickup simply never visits two of the six, and DELIVERED is rendered as "Collected" from the kind.

  **The money is the arithmetic §1 already did with the courier's lines at zero.** No delivery fee (the slice's fee is 0), no tip (dropped rather than refused — somebody who typed a tip and then switched to collection should not lose their checkout over it), service fee unchanged because the platform charges per checkout. `settle` builds the same splits and the ledger drops the zero ones, so a collection pays food − commission to the kitchen and commission + service fee to the platform and creates no `COURIER_FEE`/`TIP` entries at all. `tip` and `handoff-mode` are **409s** on a pickup rather than quiet no-ops — the tip especially, since the courier-account fallback in `OrderPayments` would otherwise pay it to the platform.

  **A `FREE_DELIVERY` promotion is refused by name on a collection**, and the test says why the rule is written rather than left to arithmetic: `discountFor` returns zero today only because the fee it is a percentage of is zero, which is an accident of the numbers. The day free delivery becomes a flat amount it would silently become money off food — precisely what §6's "a discount is an honest order line" exists to stop. An *automatic* free-delivery promotion is skipped silently instead: nobody typed anything, so there is nobody to tell.

  **Wire compatibility is total.** Every new field is additive and optional; a request without `fulfilmentKind` is a DELIVERY, and the iOS body for the common case is byte-identical to what the deployed build sends. `V41` is three `ADD COLUMN`s and a config row — it backfills nothing because the column default *is* the backfill, and it strands nothing.

  **iOS**: the restaurant page carries a "Collect in store · ready in ~N min · no fee" badge (the decision people make before choosing food, not at the end); checkout gets a Delivery/Collect pair — shown only when *every* kitchen in the cart offers it — that hides the tip picker, the delivery-fee line and the address row and replaces them with where to walk to; the tracking card shows one code per counter with the address under it and a two-step timeline; the kitchen screen gets "Ready for collection" and a numeric field that types the customer's code, with a refusal rendered under it.

  **Verified:** `mvn test` **478/478** on Corretto 21 (31 new), `ModularityTests` green — no new module edge, and the milestone is again entirely inside `delivery`. iOS `BUILD SUCCEEDED`.

  **What is deliberately not built:** the rest of M4's breadth — **scheduled orders** (SPECS §5; the note that a scheduled pickup is "the same poller with no `pickupLead`" is now cheap to act on), **modify/cancel before preparation**, **order-for-someone-else**, and **category expansion** — plus **disputes** (still Phase 4's own later slice) and a **courier rating** (fourth milestone running). Also not built: a sweep for a collection nobody ever comes for. It sits in the kitchen's queue indefinitely, which is the same hole a delivery has when a courier vanishes, and food somebody paid for is not a sweep's to throw away.

  **Unexercised.** Nothing here has run against the deployed backend. In order: a **collection end to end** (does the code reach the customer's screen and the kitchen's field, and does settlement land with no courier lines), a **wrong code** (a sentence under the field, digits left alone), and a **two-counter collection**, which is where the per-slice code earns its place.

- [x] **Phase 4 · Milestone 3 — Multi-merchant orders** ✅ **built 2026-08-04** — **deployed**: verified 2026-08-04 against the live account (task definition rev 55, image `85627ca` = `HEAD`, health 200 through the ALB), so `V40` is applied. *(The "NOT deployed" label this line carried was the **ninth** decay in this file, and it was written the same day it went stale — CI rolled the merge that carried it.)*

  **One checkout, several kitchens, one courier — and the thing M2 left as a prediction, cashed.** M2 put the pickup code on the *order* because there was exactly one merchant, and wrote down that M3 would have to split it rather than leave it alone. That is exactly what happened, and it turned out to be the spine of the milestone rather than a detail at the end of it: **the code moved down to the sub-order**, and the typed digits are now what say *which counter a courier is standing at*.

  **The parent keeps the six statuses and the courier; the slice gets one word of its own.** `sub_order.status` is CONFIRMED → PREPARING → **COLLECTED** → DELIVERED, or CANCELLED. `COLLECTED` is the only addition and it earns its place: "this kitchen's bag is in the courier's hand" is a fact about one merchant, and without it a collected slice and a still-cooking one on the same order are indistinguishable. The parent reaches DELIVERING when the *last* bag is collected. **No seventh order status, for the third milestone running** — the six words every client in the field knows are untouched.

  **The courier search waits for every kitchen and is timed to the slowest.** Not the first accept: a kitchen still deciding could add twenty minutes, and a search opened on the fast kitchen's clock is a courier standing at a counter waiting for the slow one. So the trigger is `max(readyAt) − pickupLead` once every slice has answered — still `DispatchRequest.startAfter`, still no mechanism of delivery's own, three milestones after M1 built that field for exactly this. **A rejection counts as an answer**: one kitchen saying no releases the search rather than stalling it forever behind a slice that will never accept.

  **Per-sub-order cancellation, and the invariant that keeps it safe.** A slice is the customer's to cancel exactly until its kitchen accepts (SPECS §5), and cancelling it releases **precisely that slice's share** — `subtotal − discount + its own delivery fee`. The service fee and the tip belong to the order and only move at the end, whichever end that is. `released_cents` on the parent carries the running total, and settlement refuses unless **splits + released = held**, to the cent: the same "nothing strands in escrow" check the single-merchant order had, taught about partial refunds. **The last live slice cancelling ends the order and releases the remainder in one movement** rather than a share plus a rump — two releases for one decision is two lines on a statement for one thing that happened.

  **A promotion is one merchant's campaign, so it resolves per kitchen.** The customer has one code field, so a checkout-level code is tried against every basket and applied where it lands — and refused out loud only when it lands **nowhere**, because somebody who typed WELCOME10 and got nothing still deserves to be told why. Redemption is keyed on (promotion, order) rather than order, or the second kitchen's promotion would be silently skipped because the first was recorded.

  **The multi-courier per-leg split is deliberately not built, and the cap is what makes that honest.** SPECS §5 allows courier states per leg when dispatch splits merchants across couriers. One person collects every bag here, and rather than leave that as an unstated assumption it is enforced: `delivery.order.max.merchants` (CONFIG **3**). Three counters is a trip somebody makes; five is a relay.

  **Wire compatibility is total, and that was a design constraint rather than a bonus.** The deployed app is in the field and this milestone must not strand it. The legacy `merchantId + lines` checkout still places (as a one-basket order); `OrderDto` keeps every flat field — the first slice's merchant, all the lines, the summed totals — beside the new `subOrders`; the kitchen's queue DTO is unchanged in *shape*, with `id` now naming a sub-order, which the app always treated as opaque; and the courier's job carries `stops` beside a `merchantName` that points at the **next uncollected counter**, so an older client's one-restaurant screen is still guided to the right place.

  **`OrderPlaced` is published once per kitchen** — each owner is told about their slice and its own food value, never the whole checkout, because a push naming somebody else's food is a push about an order they cannot find. Per-slice cancellation publishes **`SUB_CANCELLED`**, which is deliberately *not* one of the six: the parent has not moved, `notifications` turns it into "part of your order was refunded — the rest is still coming", and a client that has never heard the word drops it in the default arm.

  **`V40` backfills every existing order as a single-sub-order parent**, copying the per-merchant fields down verbatim — **including the pickup code**, so an order in flight when this rolls is collected with the same digits the kitchen's screen was already showing. The parent then drops what moved down (`merchant_id`, the prep fields, the code, the promotion), because a copy that is right exactly as long as every order has one merchant is a copy that is wrong from the first real use of this milestone.

  **iOS**: the cart holds several restaurants instead of wiping when you add from a second — the refusal at the cap lands on the *add*, where somebody has not yet chosen food, rather than at checkout where they have. Checkout groups by kitchen with a per-kitchen delivery fee, and the quote's discount line moves under the restaurant it landed on (a single "Discount" over two kitchens hides which one it applied to). The tracking card grows a row per kitchen, each with its own state and a **Remove** button drawn on the server's own `cancellable` — never on a status read client-side, because a rule with two definitions drifts. Courier Mode gets a "Pickup 2 of 3" chip, a leg per counter dimmed as each is collected, and a code field that clears **when a stop actually flips to collected** rather than on the tap, because M2's rule is that a refusal leaves the typed digits exactly where they are.

  **Verified:** `mvn test` **447/447** on Corretto 21 (28 new), `ModularityTests` green — no new module edge, which is worth noting: this milestone is entirely inside `delivery`. The new tests are led by the cases only a multi-kitchen order can have: a search that must not open while a kitchen is still deciding, a rejection releasing the search that was waiting on it, a slice that dies while its sibling cooks, a timeout that takes only the silent kitchen, one kitchen's code refusing to collect another's bag, a cancelled slice that must never be stamped DELIVERED, and — on the money side — a released slice shrinking exactly what settlement must account for, plus the drift check refusing to settle at all. iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED** with no new warnings.

  **Two corrections found while building, both real.** A slice lookup compared `s.getId().equals(subOrderId)`, which NPEs on any slice whose id is null — flipped to compare from the argument. And the demo/live checkout branch tested only the *first* merchant in the cart, so a cart mixing a live restaurant with a SampleData one would have posted the demo merchant to the server and 404'd; it now asks about every kitchen.

  **What is deliberately not built:** **disputes** (SPECS §5 — still the gap that means a settled order has no refund path, and still Phase 4's own later slice), a **courier rating** (third milestone running), a **share link for a delivery**, and the **per-leg multi-courier split** described above.

  **Unexercised.** Nothing here has run against the deployed backend. The paths worth driving first, in order: a **two-kitchen checkout** (do both kitchens get their own push, and does the search hold until the second accepts), a **per-slice cancel** (does exactly that slice's money come back, and does the rest of the order carry on), and a **two-counter collection** — that last one because it is where the codes stopped being one thing, and a courier who cannot collect the second bag is a courier stuck holding the first.

- [x] **Phase 4 · Milestone 2 — Handoff integrity + money** ✅ **built 2026-08-02** — **deployed**: verified 2026-08-04 against the live account (task definition rev 55, image `85627ca` = `HEAD`, health 200), so `V38` is applied. *(Ninth decay; see M3 above. Still **unexercised in production** — which is the half of "deployed" that keeps mattering.)*

  **The two taps M1 left unwitnessed.** M1 replaced a simulated courier with a real person, and in doing so made two taps load-bearing that nothing checks: *I have the food* moves the order past the point the customer can cancel, and *I handed it over* splits the escrow between a restaurant, the platform and the courier. Both were self-declared. This milestone puts a code behind each — and the codes are the smaller half of it, because the second tap is also the one that pays somebody, and until now nothing in the system could tell a delivery from a claim about one.

  **The pickup code runs the opposite way to SPECS §5, and that is the deviation to carry forward.** The spec says the courier shows a code and the merchant confirms it. Built the other way round: the kitchen's queue displays it and the courier types it. The reasoning is about incentives, not convenience. A merchant who is mid-service and forgets to tap leaves a courier standing at a counter with food they cannot mark collected and no way to proceed — the failure lands on the person who cannot fix it. A courier cannot be paid until they complete the step, so they will; and the only way for them to learn six digits sitting on a restaurant's screen is to be standing in that restaurant, which is exactly the fact the code exists to prove. The QR is the same code rendered on the kitchen's screen rather than the courier's, for the same reason.

  **A wrong code is a 200 carrying the attempts left.** Both handoff verbs return `HandoffResultDto(accepted, message, attemptsLeft, supportUnlocked, job)` and never a 4xx for a mistyped digit. Partly because a courier at a door who typed a 7 for a 1 has not made a bad request and an error banner reads as the app's fault; and partly for a mechanical reason worth writing down — **a thrown exception rolls back the attempt it was counting**. A refusal has to commit, so a refusal cannot be an exception.

  **The counting is asymmetric on purpose, and the asymmetry is the whole idea.** At the counter the merchant is standing right there, so the pickup code is a *check* rather than a gate: it never counts, never locks out (`attemptsLeft = -1`, "not counted"), and a courier who fumbles it is not locked away from food they are already holding. At the door the risk runs the other way — a courier marking an undelivered order delivered — so wrong PINs are counted, and hitting **CONFIG** 3 opens the photo fallback. **An empty PIN counts too**, which looks like a bug until you have stood outside a flat where nobody is answering: there is no PIN at that door and there never will be, so three empty taps are the honest route to the fallback built for exactly that situation.

  **The fallback is a hole, and it is documented rather than denied.** After lockout a PIN order can be completed by photographing the drop-off. That can be gamed — fail three times deliberately, photograph a doorstep — and the alternative is a courier stranded in the street with somebody's dinner because the customer is asleep, has the wrong screen open, or handed the phone to a child. The mitigations are that the attempts are recorded on the row and never reset, the photo is evidence in a dispute, and the customer sees both. **A mechanism whose failure mode is "the delivery does not happen" is not a safety mechanism.**

  **Still no new order status.** SPECS §5 says a failed PIN leaves the order `ARRIVED`; there is no such status, and M2 did not add one for the reason M1 refused to. A failed handoff leaves the order `DELIVERING`, which is precisely what it is — the courier still has the food. The six words are untouched for the second milestone running.

  **No endpoint anywhere accepts a client-supplied object key.** The proof-photo presign stamps the key it minted onto the order in the same transaction, so there is no attach step and therefore no prefix check to get wrong (which is the check the partner-document flow needs and can fail). The server also picks the content type — one `image/jpeg` means the presigned PUT and the client's header cannot disagree, the mismatch that looks like a broken upload and is really a bad signature. Retaking a photo deletes the object behind the old key, because nothing sweeps the private prefix and the alternative is a picture of a stranger's front door in the bucket forever.

  **The money half is a `dispatch` surface, and it found something the merchant case had hidden.** `GET /v1/dispatch/me/wallet` + `/me/payouts/onboarding` + `/me/payouts`, one surface for drivers and couriers because a dual-mode person has one balance, gated on holding a worker registration — `PayoutApi`'s javadoc has named dispatch for this since 2e M3. Building it exposed the asymmetry: a merchant is paid into a `MERCHANT` account with **no way to receive a top-up**, so its balance is by construction money earned; a driver or courier is paid into their own `USER AVAILABLE` bucket, the same pocket a card top-up lands in. A payout over that raw balance is the first **card→bank path** this platform has ever had. So it is capped at `min(available, max(0, lifetime earned − lifetime paid out))` over `COURIER_FEE | TIP | RIDE_FARE | REWARD`, and going over is a **409 naming the number** rather than a silent clamp. **The hole it does not close is stated rather than left to be found:** earnings are counted gross, so somebody who spends their fares and then tops up can still move card money out, bounded by a lifetime of work. What holds either way is the invariant that matters — nobody withdraws more than the platform has genuinely paid them.

  **That cap needed one new ledger read, and it had to be split in two.** `WalletApi.totalByKind` counts the **credit side only** — an entry counts when one of this owner's accounts is *receiving* — because a vehicle-verification `REWARD` moves out of one driver's `STAKING` into another person's `REWARDS`, and a two-sided sum would credit the driver who *funded* a reward with the earnings of the passenger who collected it. Lifetime paid-out is therefore **not** a sum over the `PAYOUT` ledger kind but over `payments.payout` rows: a payout Stripe refuses leaves its debit standing and reverses it with an `ADJUSTMENT`, because the ledger is append-only, so summing the kind would hold a transfer that never happened against a worker permanently. Each half lives with the module that owns the fact.

  **Three module edges arrived and none of them is new machinery.** `delivery → messaging` (the courier↔customer thread — the third consumer of the `ConversationContext` seam after a listing and a ride, which is the return on having made it generic in 2b M3), `delivery → media` (`MediaDocumentApi`: a photo of somebody's front door belongs in the private prefix beside a KYC paper, not in the world-readable one), and `dispatch → payments` (platform→platform, no cycle — `payments` has never heard of dispatch).

  **One correction made at integration, and it is worth recording because it is the shape of mistake this milestone invites.** The courier's job DTO deliberately carries neither code, and the first build over-applied that to withhold the **handoff mode** as well. Two things went wrong. The small one: a contactless order dead-ended, because the screen offered a PIN field for a PIN nobody would ever read out and the only way through was a refusal message. The large one: *"leave it at the door" is an instruction the customer gave*, in the same category as the order note, and an app that does not relay it has a courier knocking on the door of somebody who asked them not to. The mode is the **shape of the check, not the answer to it** — disclosing it costs nothing. So `CourierJobDto` carries `handoffMode`, and the courier's screen still shows the PIN field and the camera together with only the *emphasis* moving, which is what keeps a mode the app has misread from ever dead-ending a delivery.

  **iOS**: the collect tap became a six-digit field with the restaurant's name over it, a refusal rendered under the field with the typed digits left alone. The hand-over screen offers the PIN and the camera together and lets the server pick the door, promoting the camera when the customer asked for contactless or the fallback has opened. A worker wallet card in both Driver and Courier Mode — balance, lifetime earned, Stripe onboarding, preset payout amounts capped at the ceiling, and the honest sentence when the ceiling is below the balance. Payout amounts are chips rather than a keypad, which is the wallet's own precedent and the one-handed constraint. The kitchen's queue shows the pickup code at 34pt mono under "READ THIS TO THE COURIER" — it is the one thing on that screen a person reads aloud, so it looks like it.
