On the customer's side: the PIN, a three-way *Hand to me · Leave at door · No code* control that reverts on refusal, a live "Message courier" button, and the proof photo on the delivered sheet. It also fixed something older: `PartnerDashboardView` had been *calling* `walletNotice` since Phase 3 and never drawing it, so every payout result would have been silent.

  **Verified:** `mvn test` **399/399** on Corretto 21, `ModularityTests` green with all three new edges. **58 new tests** — 28 `HandoffTests`, 11 `HandoffModeTests`, 15 `WorkerMoneyTests`, 4 `EarningsAggregateTests`, led by the cases the codes exist for: a wrong pickup code that does not count, a PIN mismatch that counts and unlocks at exactly the maximum, an empty PIN counting, the photo path refusing without an upload, the fallback after lockout, a refusal moving neither the status nor the money, and — on the money side — a top-up raising the balance but not the ceiling. iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED** with no new warnings.

  **What is deliberately not built:** a **courier rating** (still — the order rating is a judgement on food, and M1's reasoning has not changed), **QR scanning** (the code renders as one on the kitchen's screen; typing is the path, and a camera the Simulator does not have is a poor primary), **disputes** (SPECS §5's `delivery.dispute`, which is what a proof photo is evidence *for* and remains a later Phase 4 slice), and a **share link for a delivery**.

  **Unexercised, and this milestone's version of it is specific.** Nothing here has run against the deployed backend, and no simulator pass was possible while the endpoints did not exist. The paths worth driving first, in order: a **wrong pickup code** (the message lands in place and the typed digits survive), **three empty PIN submissions** reaching the fallback, and a **PHOTO order end to end** — that last one because it is the exact path the first iOS build dead-ended, so it is the one with a demonstrated ability to be wrong.

- [x] **Phase 4 · Milestone 1 — Real couriers** ✅ **built + deployed 2026-08-02** (task definition **rev 45**, image `e333f24`, `V35` applied and prod is at schema **v37** — confirmed against the live account, having been labelled "NOT deployed" until then, the **eighth** decay in this file). *(The labels here decay — check the running image and `git fetch` before believing any of them.)*

  **⚠️ Read before rolling this: the deploy cancels any delivery order still in flight.** `V35` deliberately touches none of them, because a migration cannot release an escrow hold and a row marked CANCELLED with the money still held is the one genuinely unacceptable state to leave behind. `OrderTimeoutJob` collects them instead on its first passes after startup — a non-terminal order with no `acceptedAt` is exactly what an order stranded by the deletion of the fulfilment job looks like — and cancels *and refunds* each one through the same path a restaurant that never answers takes. That is the correct outcome and it is worth knowing rather than discovering. The delivery catalog is empty today (every merchant went SUSPENDED in the 2026-08-01 cleanup), so in practice that set is almost certainly empty.

  **The swap 2b M4 designed, two months of milestones later.** Delivery shipped in 2b M4 with a real state machine, real events, a real API — and a **simulated** fulfilment: `OrderFulfilmentJob` read a clock and walked every order to wherever `DeliveryTimeline` said it should be, with a kitchen that always took twenty seconds and a courier picked from a hardcoded roster of four names. The bet written down at the time was that only the *actor* was fake, so dispatch would replace one job and nothing else. **It came out exactly that way**: the six order statuses, both domain events and every customer-facing endpoint are unchanged, and what was deleted is one job and one class of durations.

  **`dispatch` did not change by a single line, which is the point.** `delivery` hangs its courier assignment off the same `DispatchAssigned` / `DispatchExhausted` events `travel` has used since 3 M3 — a prediction sitting in `travel`'s own listener as a comment ("*delivery will hang its courier assignment off the same two events in Phase 4 M1 without dispatch changing at all*"). Not a verb, not a constant, not a `JobKind` branch. That is the entire return on events rather than callbacks, and it is the second time a platform module has absorbed a new consumer for free.

  **Deleting a simulation costs a surface, and the surface is the kitchen.** The fulfilment job was the only thing that ever moved an order, so before a courier can be real a *restaurant* has to be: `/v1/delivery/merchants/mine/orders` with accept (carrying a prep estimate), reject and ready. `MerchantManagementService` had said in as many words why that screen did not exist — "*giving the kitchen a button that the job would immediately overrule is worse than not having one*" — and that reason expired with the job. The prep estimate is load-bearing rather than cosmetic: it is the only thing in the system that knows when food will be ready, and the customer's countdown, the courier's pickup time and the moment the search starts are all derived from it. It falls back to a configured default rather than failing validation, because **an accept that can be refused is an accept somebody in a kitchen does not make**.

  **The courier trigger is `DispatchRequest.startAfter`, and nothing else.** SPECS §3's "approaching readiness" reads like a mechanism and is not one: a courier search that must not start until the kitchen is nearly done is simply *a job whose first wave is in the future*. Dispatch built the field in M1 for exactly this and no caller had ever used it; `readyAt − pickupLead` (7 min, config) is the whole implementation. Too early and a courier stands at a counter unpaid; too late and the food does.

  **The state machine did not grow a word, deliberately.** Two states were tempting and both are wrong. "The food is ready" is a *timestamp*, because the customer's dinner is still coming and their screen should still say so. "Cooking, and nobody has taken the delivery" is a **`courier_search`** column (NONE / SEARCHING / ASSIGNED / FAILED) beside the status, because it is a problem *with* an order rather than a stage *of* one — and a seventh status is a word every client in the field has to learn, for information one of them can act on and the rest can only mis-render.

  **An exhausted search is flagged, never retried and never auto-cancelled.** Dispatch's job row is unique on (kind, ref) by design, so a second search would need a platform verb to re-open a closed job — and it is the same question to the same empty city, since every ring in range was already asked for the whole request window. Auto-cancelling would throw away food that may already be made, which is a decision for the people involved rather than for a sweep. So both sides are told: the customer sees "no courier picked this one up — cancel for a full refund if you'd rather not wait", and the kitchen sees it before the food does.

  **A restaurant that never answers is the failure a simulation could not have**, and `OrderTimeoutJob` is what is left of the job that used to drive everything — inverted: it drives nothing, and the only thing a clock is still entitled to decide is that nobody did. After ten minutes (config) the order is cancelled *and released*. The same query collects the orders stranded by this milestone's own deploy, which is why **`V35` deliberately cancels nothing itself**: releasing an escrow hold is a ledger movement, and a row marked CANCELLED with the money still held is the one genuinely unacceptable outcome a migration could leave behind.

  **The courier gets paid, and it really was one line.** 2e M3 could have folded the delivery fee and the tip into commission; it settled them to the platform under `COURIER_FEE` and `TIP` instead, precisely so this day would be a payee change rather than an archaeology project across a year of platform revenue. It was. The platform fallback stays for the orders that predate this — not a soft failure mode, but because a split has to name an account and refusing to settle an old order would strand its money in escrow.

  **The courier's own REST surface is two verbs**, which is the module boundary showing. Going online, reporting a position, seeing an offer and accepting it are **dispatch's** and identical for a driver and a courier — Courier Mode has had all of it since 3 M1 and simply never had anything to be offered. What is new here is "I have the food" and "I handed it over". Both are deliberate taps rather than geofence inferences: being *at* a counter is not the same as having what is on it, and a delivery confirmed by proximity is a delivery somebody can be paid for without doing.

  **`OrderPlaced` finally got a consumer, and it needed a field.** Nobody had to be told an order existed before, because a timeline started cooking on its own; a kitchen that is never told has an order it can only time out. So the event carries `merchantOwnerId` — a fact `delivery` already holds from provisioning — and `notifications` pushes to the owner.

  **Two things that are now measured rather than invented.** `courierProgress` is the courier's reported position projected onto the restaurant→door line, falling back to 0 when any of the three is unknown; the old timeline drew a pin that slid along smoothly whatever the courier was doing, including standing still. And the ETA at checkout is the restaurant's own advertised time until they accept, then `readyAt + dropoff`.

  **What is deliberately not built:** a **courier rating** — the order rating is not passed to `dispatch.complete`, because a two-star for a cold burger is about the food as much as the trip and must not land on the record of whoever cycled it across town; **handoff codes and PINs** (Phase 4 M2, SPECS §5); a **share link for a delivery** (`delivery` implements `ShareableResource` and nothing else changes); and a **second courier search** for an order nobody took.

  **iOS**: the kitchen's queue on the merchant dashboard, above everything else on it — accept with a segmented prep picker rather than a text field, because this is tapped one-handed and a keyboard is how a 5 that meant 50 gets in. Courier Mode gained the other half it never had: the delivery being carried sits above the online radar (which hides, since a radar saying "looking for requests" over an order somebody is holding is the app contradicting itself), with the two taps and the customer's note, which is the one piece of free text a courier genuinely needs. On the customer's side the tracking card now shows a real person and a real position, the no-courier state says so, and a cancellation finally explains itself — under the simulation nothing could cancel an order but the customer, so the screen simply vanishing was never wrong before.

  **Verified:** `mvn test` **320/320** (`ModularityTests` green with the new `delivery → dispatch` edge, plus 27 new tests — 19 on the fulfilment path, led by the cases a timeline could not reach: a restaurant that says no, one that never answers, a search nobody takes, a courier assigned to an order that was cancelled underneath them, and a redelivered assignment that must not stand a courier down mid-delivery — a real bug this test found; 5 on where the fee and the tip land; 2 structural, pinning `REQUIRES_NEW` on both listener entry points, which is the M3 production defect turned into a rule for the second module to hit it). iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**.

- [x] **Phase 3 · Milestone 5 — Community vehicle verification + the safety pack** ✅ **deployed 2026-08-02** (task definition **rev 37**, image `9fc9394`, `V33` applied and prod is at schema **v33** — 34 migrations validated, health 200 through the ALB; no `cdk deploy`, since the milestone adds no secret, no env var and no IAM action) — the milestone that closes Phase 3's core, and the first one whose subject is not a transaction.

  **The stake finally does the job it was collected for.** M2 took $30 off every driver and locked it in their own STAKING bucket, and SPECS §4 has always said what it funds: *verification*. This is it. On the first completed trip in an APPROVED-but-unverified car, the passenger is asked five questions — was the driver the person in the app, did the car match its photos, did the plate match, did it seem roadworthy, did anything feel like a scam — and all five yesses earn the vehicle its badge and pay that passenger **out of the driver's stake**. A reviewer only ever saw a photograph of a registration document; a passenger saw the actual car with the actual driver in it, which is the only check in the system that can catch a swapped plate.

  **The vehicle had to be made to travel, and it travels the way the plate already did.** A verification has to land on the car the passenger rode in, not on whatever the driver has made active by the time they answer. So `partner` now pushes a **`VehicleRef`** — id, category, label, plate, verified — down to the dispatch registration at provisioning and on every vehicle change; dispatch hands it back on an `Assignment`; `travel` snapshots it onto the ride at CONFIRMED beside the plate it already snapshotted. `travel → partner` still does not exist. The badge rides in the same record for the same reason a plate does: **a badge nobody sees is not a badge**, and where it is seen is a rider on a kerb.

  **Four rules keep the mechanism honest, and each is an answer to a way it could be gamed or simply rot.** *One invitation at a time, one per trip, three per car* — a vehicle that asked every passenger would earn a badge meaning "somebody eventually said yes", and after three attempts it simply stays APPROVED, which is not a failure state. *The same passenger is never asked twice, and neither is the driver* — otherwise one friend's five trips are a badge. *All five, or it is a rejection, and a missing answer counts as no* — each question is a different way the car could be wrong, and an older client omitting a field must not verify a car by omission. *Silence is not an accusation* — an expired invitation flags nobody, because a passenger who never opened the app has said nothing about the car. A sweep marks them, and without it the "is an invitation still out?" check would find a three-week-old row forever and the fall-through to the next passenger would never happen.

  **A "no" flags the car and takes its driver off the road, in one transaction** — the same rule the papers-expiry sweep follows, because a flag that leaves work arriving is a flag that did nothing. Every verdict is attached to the vehicle on the review surface, so a moderator can read *why* rather than seeing a state. **The reward is capped at what is left of the stake and recorded on the application** (`reward_paid_minor`, folded into `remainingStakeMinor` beside the KYC fee): without the column a driver whose stake has paid three rewards still looks fully staked, and a release would hand back money that is already somebody else's. A driver with nothing left pays nothing and the badge still stands — it is about the car, not the balance.

  **A public tracking link is a module, not a table.** SPECS §10 spec'd `share_token(kind, refId, token, expiresAt)` as a row in the trust & safety work. What it has to be is a module, because the payload must be **rendered on every read** or a "live tracking link" shows where the car was when the link was made — which is a screenshot with a URL. So the new platform **`share`** module stores the kind, the id and the secret, and asks the owning vertical for the view through **`ShareableResource`**: the fourth instance of the `ConversationGuard` / `ModeratableContent` / `WorkerEligibility` shape, and the same direction. A kind nothing on the classpath renders is simply not shareable, and `delivery` can share an order later without `share` learning what one is.

  **The token is the whole authentication, so the payload is thin and every failure is identical.** The people a trip is shared with are exactly the people with no account here, so making them sign up first is how a safety feature stops being used. That makes two things load-bearing: 192 bits from `SecureRandom`, never derived from the ride id; and a payload chosen by asking what a stranger who found the link in a group chat should see — first names, a plate, a position, an ETA, a destination. No handle, no profile id, no phone number, no fare. Expired, revoked, unknown kind and never-existed all return the **same 404**, because a stream of guesses that can tell them apart is a stream of guesses that is learning something. Minting is **idempotent** per (kind, ref, owner, reason) — two secrets for one trip would leave the first un-revokable — and only ever **extends** a link, since shortening one silently cuts off the person already watching. A link outlives its trip by a grace, because one that dies at the kerb dies at the one moment somebody is looking at it.

  **SOS: the flag is written first, it cannot be un-raised, and the server never dials.** The ride row is flushed before a link is minted or an event published, so an alarm is never lost to a downstream outage — and there is no cancel, because the flag is what an operator sees and an alarm that can be talked away is one somebody under pressure will be talked into removing. What comes back is the local emergency number (**112** by default — it works across the EU and most GSM networks, which is the right answer when we don't know where somebody is) for the *phone* to call. A backend placing an emergency call on somebody's behalf is a claim about integration with emergency services this system has not earned, and SPECS §10's "no PSAP integration claimed" is the same instinct taken literally.

  **Emergency contacts are a phone number first and a GojoGo account second**, which is the opposite of what an app-centric design does. The person you would call in an emergency is usually not a user of the app you happen to be sitting in, so a list that only held accounts would be empty at the moment it mattered. A **linked** contact gets a push instantly; everybody else is reached by the phone's own message composer, pre-filled with wording the *server* wrote — the only channel that works while SNS SMS is still sandboxed (#3 below), and the one whose message arrives from a number the recipient recognises. They live in `profile`, not `travel`: an emergency contact is a fact about a person. **Deleting an account takes the list with it** — other people's names and phone numbers are exactly what a deletion should carry away, and unlike a post nobody's thread has a hole in it when they go.

  **The operator surface is read-only, deliberately.** `GET /v1/travel/admin/sos`, on the same terms as partner review and the moderation queue. There is no "resolve" button because there is no honest thing for one to mean: a trip in trouble is dealt with by a person picking up a phone, and a status changing in a queue would only make it look handled. The local console ([`tools/admin-console`](tools/admin-console/README.md)) grew the screen, so a raised alarm is something a person can actually see.

  **iOS**: Share and SOS sit above Cancel on the trip sheet, because the person who needs them is not looking for the cancel button. Share is one tap and reversible; **SOS is one tap and a confirmation** — an alarm raised by accident is a person explaining themselves to their family at midnight, and one that needs three taps is an alarm nobody raises in time. Once raised, the sheet turns into the emergency number under a thumb, the contacts we couldn't reach with a "Text them" button each, and the live link to send anybody else. Emergency contacts live in Settings → Safety, **above** the block list: it is the one control there you are meant to set up *before* anything goes wrong. The verification card appears on the travel screen (the one screen the person who can answer it is already on) and the sheet says plainly, under a "no", that this takes the driver off the road until somebody reviews it. The verified badge sits beside the plate, which is what it is a claim about. Every sheet presents from the travel screen alone — one owner per AppState-driven dialog, the rule this codebase has paid for before.

  **Verified:** `mvn test` **293/293** (`ModularityTests` green with the new `share` module, the `travel → share` SPI edge and `partner`'s listener on `travel.TripCompleted`, plus 42 new tests — 14 on who gets asked and what one "no" does, 10 on share tokens led by "expired and nonsense look identical", 13 on SOS and what a stranger sees, 5 on the contact list). iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**. Writing the contact-list test found a real bug before it shipped: a new contact was constructed and flushed but never `save`d, so it would have been dropped on the floor in production.

  **Not built, and deliberately:** the optional passenger **photos** on a verification (the five answers were the blocking half; the private-prefix upload path already exists, so adding them needs no migration), **share links for content or stories**, **SOS on a delivery** (the `share` module is generic — `delivery` implements `ShareableResource` whenever it wants one), and **universal-link routing** for `https://gojogo.app/s/{token}` — the endpoint is live and that path is what the route will map to, but the apple-app-site-association file is an account decision, so a link opens in a browser today.

  **Deployed by CI**, from the push of `9fc9394` — no `./scripts/deploy-backend.sh` was run by hand. `V33` created the `share` schema, `partner.vehicle_verification`, `profile.emergency_contact` and `share.share_token`, added columns to `travel.ride`, `dispatch.worker` and `partner.partner_account`, and seeded eight config rows; the startup log validates **34 migrations** and reports schema **33**, and health is 200 through the ALB, which is a real database round-trip. **Nothing about it has been exercised in production yet** — see the resume notes for which paths have never run.

- [x] **Phase 3 · Milestone 4 — Ride-hailing tokens** ✅ **deployed and prod-verified 2026-08-01** (task definition **rev 35**, image `f063af3`, `V32` applied at startup and prod is at schema **v32**; no `cdk deploy` needed for the milestone itself) — the milestone that gives transport a business model. M3 settled a fare as one rider→driver transfer with **no commission**, which is the vision's model rather than a gap: the driver keeps every cent, and pays a **token** for the right to accept the job. Until this, a ride earned the platform nothing at all.

  **A token is a fixed number of minor units, and the TOKENS bucket holds money like every other bucket.** The ledger has exactly one amount per entry, so a bucket that counted tokens would make a purchase an entry whose two sides are in different units — which is not double-entry bookkeeping, it is two single-sided writes with a hyphen between them. `payments.token.value.minor` is the only conversion in the system, and it lives behind a new public **`TokenApi`** whose callers speak in whole tokens, so no vertical holds a second copy of what one is worth.

  **The price list is split across two modules, and that is the milestone's main boundary call.** `payments.token_pack` is a size and a price — no vertical vocabulary at all. What a *ride* costs is `travel.ride_token_price`, keyed by vehicle category and distance band. SPECS §4 puts both in `payments`; the second moved because it is keyed on a `dispatch.VehicleCategory` and a distance in metres, vocabulary a money module has no business learning and could only store as strings it cannot check. Beside `travel.pricing_config` it is what it actually is — **the other half of the same price change**: what the rider pays and what the driver pays now move together. **Bands are upper bounds, not ranges** (the cheapest `up_to_metres` that covers the trip, inclusive at the top, with a catch-all per category), because a (min, max) pair can be left with a gap and a gap is a ride that silently costs nothing. Nothing covering a distance falls to **zero**, not to the dearest band: free trips get reported, overcharged ones get left over.

  **A pack is bought out of the wallet, not off a card.** SPECS §1 says "Stripe → TOKENS credit at pack rate"; 2e M3 had already settled that card money in this platform only ever arrives as a wallet top-up, and a second Stripe path would mean a second webhook, a second failure mode and a second refund story. So a purchase is AVAILABLE → TOKENS, and a short wallet is a **402 carrying the shortfall** — the same one-tap top-up the $30 stake uses. **A pack's bonus is a second entry funded by `PLATFORM`**: twelve tokens for the price of eleven is eleven from the driver and one from GojoGo, because a discount credited with no debit is a ledger that quietly stops adding up until the nightly audit finds it.

  **Dispatch filters, and still knows nothing about money.** SPECS §4 says a driver below the minimum "is filtered out by dispatch, not blocked from the app" — but dispatch's offer book asks "will you do this job" and must no more grow an opinion about a token than it did about a fare in M3. So it defines **`WorkerEligibility`** and `travel` implements it: the third instance of the `ConversationGuard` / `ModeratableContent` shape, and a dispatch module with no implementations on the classpath behaves exactly as it did before. It is asked **per job**, because a short hop and a cross-city trip cost different numbers of tokens and a single "can this person work" flag would have to assume the worst. It runs **after the ranking and before the wave limit**, so an ineligible driver is reached past rather than shrinking a wave of five to one; the stream is lazy, so it is only asked until the wave is full.

  **Being filtered out is completely silent, which is the milestone's real risk.** Dispatch simply skips them — no error, no offer, nothing — so a driver goes online, waits, and concludes the app is broken. `GET /v1/travel/tokens` is the answer, and it lives in `travel` because only the module that prices a ride can say whether a balance is enough to take one. It returns the sentence; the app never composes one.

  **The charge never blocks a confirmation, and a cancellation always returns it.** Prevention lives at matching, so reaching CONFIRMED unable to pay needs the balance to move between the offer and the handshake, which the busy flag already prevents; refusing there would take a car away from a rider who has been promised one, over the driver's finances, and say so. And **tokens follow the trip, not the fault**: SPECS §4 refunds only on a driver-fault cancellation, which would make cancelling a revenue line for the platform and charge a driver for a rider's change of mind — a driver who accepts and cancels repeatedly is already priced, because dispatch counts every one against them.

  **Two things the build added that the spec does not name.** A **welcome grant** at approval (5 tokens, config), without which this milestone ships a silent stop: an approval would hand somebody a registration, an empty balance and a Driver Mode where nothing ever arrives. And a **correction to the wallet statement** — it used to drop every movement between a person's own buckets as bookkeeping, which is right for a hold and wrong for a stake or a token purchase, both of which take money out of what you can spend. Own-bucket lines are now shown, signed from the AVAILABLE side; a pocket also has its own history signed from *itself*, because the same purchase is money out on a wallet and eleven tokens in on a token screen.

  **iOS**: a Ride tokens screen in Driver Mode — balance in tokens rather than money, the packs, what each category costs by distance, and the token half of the statement counted in tokens. The card that opens it carries the server's refusal sentence, which makes it the only place in the product that explains an empty Driver Mode. Buying is one tap, and a refusal turns into a top-up for exactly the shortfall. It is drawn only for a **registered** driver: a currency for a job somebody hasn't been approved for is a screen that raises a question the app can't answer. Presented from the dashboard cover alone, with local state — one owner per dialog, the rule this codebase has paid for before.

  **Verified:** `mvn test` **249/249** (`ModularityTests` green with the new `travel → dispatch` SPI edge and `partner`/`travel` → `payments.TokenApi`, plus 37 new tests — 6 on band selection, where every failure is silent; 10 on charging and the eligibility veto; 10 on purchase arithmetic, led by "the bonus never lands without the purchase"; 5 on statement signing; 3 on the wave reaching past an ineligible driver; 3 on the ride wiring). iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**.

  **Prod E2E, run 2026-08-01 — the first time any of Phase 3 has been exercised against a real backend.** Three test accounts (`gojogo-m4-{driver,rider,merchant}@example.com`), an approved driver with a papered vehicle, and an approved restaurant. What it proved, in order:

  - **The welcome grant is the difference between a working Driver Mode and a dead one.** Before approval: `canWork: false`, *"You're out of ride tokens, so you won't be offered trips."* After: 5 tokens, `canWork: true`. Without the grant this milestone would have shipped a silent stop for every driver it ever approved.
  - **The stake and the fee behave exactly as M2 designed them**, which had also never run in prod: AVAILABLE 5000 → 2000 with 3000 locked in STAKING, then the $5 ID check taken **out of the stake** (3000 → 2500) and not out of the wallet.
  - **`requiredDocuments` changes shape with the vendor.** With Sumsub off, DRIVER asked for `ID_FRONT` + `SELFIE` and RESTAURANT for `ID_FRONT` + `BUSINESS_LICENSE`; with it on, the identity kinds drop out. The 2b M6 design, confirmed live.
  - **Dispatch offered the trip in ~4 seconds** and the eligibility veto passed a driver holding 5 tokens for a 1-token trip.
  - **Both confirmation paths**: a driver countering 645 → 800 and the rider accepting (which calls `DispatchApi.assign`), and a driver accepting in dispatch at the asking price. The second is the one that found the bug.
  - **Two token bands, charged correctly**: a 2825 m trip cost **1** token (≤3000 band) and a 5927 m trip cost **2** (≤10000 band).
  - **The fare is one transfer with no commission**: rider 3000 → 2200, driver 2000 → 2800, exactly the agreed 800. The platform's entire revenue on that ride was the one token.
  - **A cancellation returns the tokens** — 2 → 4 on a **rider**-fault cancel, which is the deliberate deviation from SPECS §4 — while the $2 cancellation fee still went **to the driver**.
  - **The statement correction is visible**: the $30 stake and every token purchase now appear as lines. Under the old rule a wallet dropped by thirty dollars with nothing anywhere to explain it.
  - **`tokenCost` is null to the rider** and populated for the driver, as intended.
  - **Refused correctly**: a second live ride (409), an offer accepted after its 45s TTL (409), and a fare the rider could not cover (402 naming a $9.71 shortfall — M3's balance-check-at-request, also never previously run).

  **Not proven:** the Sumsub verdict path — this run used the manual document-review fallback, so the vendor's own verdict has still never been exercised end to end and the first real applicant will be its first full test. Nor the low-balance push actually reaching a phone. The driver did drop to 2 tokens (under the threshold of 3) so `TokensRanOut` fired, but these accounts have no registered device — real APNs delivery is still "Needs YOU" #1.

  **How the approvals were possible, and what was changed to do it.** Both applications gate submission on a verified identity, and prod runs Sumsub with **production** credentials — putting a fabricated identity through a live, billed KYC/AML vendor was not an acceptable way to test. So Sumsub was deliberately deployed **unconfigured** for about fifteen minutes (task definition rev 33, no `SUMSUB_*` secrets; the app falls back to its own document-review path, which is exactly what that fallback is for), the two applications were reviewed and approved by hand on the break-glass token, and the production secret — never read, never rewritten — was restored at rev 34. Startup log confirms `production environment, level 'id-and-liveness', webhooks verified`, and all nine secrets are present.

  **The test data was cleared the same day**, as far as the product's own surfaces allow — and what could *not* be removed is worth knowing, because it is a design decision rather than an oversight:

  - **Gone:** the three Cognito accounts (`DELETE /v1/me` first, which disabled sign-in and started the 30-day clock — incidentally the first live exercise of 2e M5's deletion path — then removed from the pool outright), and the six placeholder documents from the private S3 prefix.
  - **Suspended, not deleted:** `Dar Zellij` and `Amina Driver`, plus **`Admin Test Kitchen 1321`**, a leftover from an earlier session that was sitting **approved and in the live catalog** — found during this cleanup. All four partner accounts are now SUSPENDED and the delivery catalog is empty. Suspension is the reversible lever: approving again restores any of them.
  - **Cannot be removed, by design:** `delivery.merchant` and `dispatch.worker` rows have no delete path (suspension is the intended off switch); the three `profile` rows are tombstoned rather than dropped, which is the whole content plan from 2e M5; and **the ledger is untouched on purpose** — a financial record is not ours to erase, so the $80 of Stripe test-mode top-ups, the driver's locked 2500 stake and its 4 tokens all remain as history. `travel.ride` rows likewise stay.
  - **Left alone deliberately:** four older `gojogo-*@example.com` accounts and ten private documents from previous sessions' verifications. They are somebody else's fixtures and PROGRESS references them; say the word and they go too.

- [x] **Phase 3 · Milestone 3 — The ride loop** ✅ **deployed 2026-08-01** (task definition **rev 29**, image `19a1107`, `V31` applied at startup; no `cdk deploy` needed) — new **`travel`** vertical (`travel` schema, `V31`), and the first thing in this system that ever asks `dispatch` for anything: M1 built the matching, M2 built the drivers, and until now nothing had given either of them a job.

  **What makes a ride different from a delivery order is one word: negotiation.** A delivery is priced by the server and paid; a ride has a price two people agree on. So fares live in `travel`, **offers are rows**, and `dispatch` — whose offer book asks "will you do this job" and has no opinion about money — must not grow one. A driver who wants a different fare **declines in dispatch and counters here**; when the rider accepts, travel calls the new **`DispatchApi.assign`**, and the job row is still the arbiter, so a counter accepted a second after somebody took the trip at the asking price loses exactly as a second driver would. A counteroffer is a row rather than a column because there are two rounds and both sides make them — "they asked 45, I came back with 38, they took it" is a sequence — and because it lets a rider hold three drivers' prices at once and pick one.

  **Three fares, because they are three facts**: what the engine suggested, what the rider put on the table, and what was agreed — **frozen at CONFIRMED and never recomputed**, since a price that moves after a handshake is not a handshake. The other two are kept so "you charged 12 when the app said 10" has an answer.

  **Money moves once, at completion, and the driver keeps all of it.** Rides carry no commission (SPECS §1 — the platform's revenue is M4's tokens), so it is a single transfer under a new **`RIDE_FARE`** ledger kind rather than a split; "Transfer" next to a trip is a statement line nobody can read. SPECS gives rides **no escrow**, which leaves a hole this fills with a **balance check at request and again at confirmation** — a fare nobody can pay should be refused while the trip can still not happen, rather than after somebody has been driven across a city. A cancellation fee applies only once the driver was actually **on the way**, is paid to *them* rather than the platform, and is **waived rather than enforced** when the wallet is empty: trapping a rider in a ride they cannot pay to leave is worse than a driver missing two dollars.

  **The car a rider looks for on a kerb** lives in `partner`, a vertical — so it is **pushed down** to the dispatch registration at provisioning and whenever the active vehicle changes, rather than read across. That keeps `travel → partner` from existing at all, and makes the plate a snapshot: a receipt still names the car that collected you after the driver sells it.

  **Ratings are mutual and blind until both are in** — a driver who can read a one-star before writing their own is being invited to answer it. **Routing is straight-line × a road factor**, not Mapbox: the server has no Mapbox token, wiring one is an account decision, and a suggestion the two parties then negotiate away from does not need a routing bill per quote. The seam is one method.

  **iOS**: the travel screen's phases already described a real journey and were being driven by a timer — they are now driven by a ride row. Real quotes for every category (the app has never computed a fare and doesn't start), a real request, **counteroffer cards on the matching sheet** with Accept and dismiss, a live driver position, real cancel and real rating. The simulation stays for an account with no backend, branching on `backendConnected` rather than a debug flag.

  **Verified:** `mvn test` **212/212** (`ModularityTests` green — `travel → dispatch`/`payments`/`messaging`/`profile`/`config` all public-API-only — plus 29 new tests: 11 on the arithmetic every rider sees, and 18 on the ways haggling goes silently wrong — a driver bidding on a trip nobody offered them, two live prices from one person, a counter taken after the job was gone leaving the ride untouched and the offer still live). iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**. **Deployed** as rev 29 / image `19a1107`, `V31` applied at startup and health 200 through the ALB.

  **No prod E2E, and it needs a person.** The loop cannot be exercised without an **approved driver** — which means somebody in the `platform-admin` group approving a DRIVER application with a papered vehicle ("Needs YOU" #5). Until then `/v1/travel/quote` and a request work, and every ride simply expires with nobody to offer it to. Two of my own test expectations were wrong here and the code was right: a fare I'd computed ignored the minimum-fare floor, and an offer sitting exactly on the ceiling is allowed rather than refused.

- [x] **Phase 3 · Milestone 2 — Driver onboarding completes** ✅ **deployed 2026-08-01** (task definition **rev 27**, image `b3901f6`, `V30` applied at startup; no `cdk deploy` needed) — M1 gave an approved driver somewhere to land; what sat between "I'd like to drive" and an approval worth granting was **a convincing mime**. The stake page slept 1.6 seconds and charged nothing, "submit" inserted a role into a local `Set`, and the vehicle form — make, model, plate, and two tiles you tapped to *claim* you'd photographed the papers — went nowhere. That last one is the same pattern the identity half lost when Sumsub arrived, for the same reason: a checkbox saying a photo was taken is worse than no photo, because it looks like evidence.

  **The stake is a ledger movement and it stays the applicant's money** — AVAILABLE → their own **STAKING** bucket, which is what `payments`' buckets are for: out of circulation without changing hands, so giving it back is a *release* and not a refund, and M5's verification reward will be a transfer out of it rather than an invention. The columns on the application record that a movement happened; they are deliberately **not** a second copy of the balance. It **gates submission, not approval** (SPECS §4 — staking funds verification, so it precedes the human's time), the **KYC fee comes out of the stake** rather than the wallet (otherwise somebody can stake and leave nothing to check them with), and every idempotency key is derived from a fact already on the row — for the fee, the total charged so far, so a retry collapses and a genuine resubmission does not. A **zero fee writes no entry at all**. **Rejection and withdrawal release the stake; a suspension does not** — keeping it on a rejection would make refusing people profitable, and returning it on a suspension defeats the thing it exists to fund.

  **Vehicles live in `partner`, not `dispatch`** (`V30`): a vehicle is a claim a human reviews, and dispatch needs one fact out of it. **Editing the plate or the category un-approves it** — an edited plate describes a different car, and that is the exact shape of the fraud this check exists to catch — while repainting it does not. **Exactly one active vehicle**, held by a partial unique index rather than by the service, since "activate this one" is two writes. **Plates are unique per region**, never globally. And papers expire: a nightly-ish sweep with a 7-day grace flags a lapsed vehicle and **suspends its driver if it was the active one**, because the day a certificate ran out is not an event anything else would ever notice. **A driver needs a vehicle; a courier does not** — a bicycle has no registration to show. Provisioning now names the **active vehicle's category**, replacing M1's null-and-default, and a flagged vehicle names none rather than one dispatch must not send work for.

  **iOS: the flow stops lying.** Real application, real stake against the wallet (the page reads the server's amount, the real balance and the shortfall — which turns an empty wallet into a top-up for exactly the missing amount), real private uploads for registration and insurance, and a done page that says **submitting queues you** rather than "you're now a driver". `partnerRoles` now comes from `/v1/me/roles` reading the dispatch registry, so the app cannot disagree with the platform about who may work. **Driver Mode is live for a registered driver** — availability, position reporting at the server's interval, offers, accept/decline — and stays the local demo for everybody else, branching on *the registry* rather than a debug flag, because the one thing that must not happen is a real driver seeing an invented offer. Two deliberate stops: the offer card shows **no fare** (dispatch never sees money; "—", not "$0.00"), and a real acceptance **ends there**, because what happens next belongs to a vertical and none publishes a job yet.

  **Verified:** `mvn test` **183/183** (`ModularityTests` green with the new `partner → payments` / `partner → config` public-API edges, plus 27 new tests — 12 on money that must *not* move, 15 on the vehicle rules, led by "changing the plate un-approves, changing the colour doesn't"). iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**.

- [x] **Phase 3 · Milestone 1 — The platform `dispatch` module** ✅ **deployed 2026-08-01** (task definition **rev 27**, image `b3901f6`, `V29` applied at startup) — two things had been waiting on this module *by name*: a `DRIVER`/`COURIER` approval has refused with a 409 since 2b M6 because there was nowhere for an approved driver to land, and GojoDelivery's courier is a name and a rating **invented by a scheduled job**.

  New platform **`dispatch` module** (`dispatch` schema, `V29__dispatch.sql`) that knows a kind, an opaque id, a place and a vehicle category — **and no money**. A `DispatchRequest` carrying a fare would make dispatch a party to the transaction, and then two modules would own what a trip is worth. That narrowness is what lets one module serve both verticals: a rider wanting a car and a restaurant wanting a courier are the same search with different filters. **One registry with a `kind`**, not `driver` and `courier` side by side — a dual-mode person is one human with two rows, which makes "an accepted job in either mode makes you busy in both" a one-line rule instead of a cross-table invariant, and is why a position report fans out to every registration they hold.

  **Presence is Postgres, not Redis, and that is a stated deviation from SPECS §3.** Redis GEO is right when thousands of workers are moving; there are zero, an ElastiCache cluster bills by the hour regardless, and the read is "available workers of this kind within N km" over a table already narrowed by kind and status. So the database runs a **bounding box** and Java measures the real distance over what comes back — one repository method to swap later, no caller affected. The box is the one piece of arithmetic whose bugs are *invisible* (a window a fraction too small silently loses the nearest driver, and nothing logs that), so it is padded and tested all the way round the ring — which caught a real ulp-width gap at bearing 180°.

  **Ranking is a pure function** because nothing on this machine can execute SQL, and "who gets this trip" is the last logic that should first run in production. Proximity 60 / rating 25 / **idle time 15** — the third is the one a simpler system omits, and without it the nearest driver takes every job on a quiet afternoon. Pinned in both directions: an hour of waiting beats a driver about as close, and does **not** beat one two kilometres nearer, because fairness between drivers is a tiebreak and the passenger in the street outranks it.

  **First acceptance wins and the database is the arbiter** — the service checks the job state, and a JPA `@Version` settles the case the check cannot see, since two accepts a millisecond apart both pass an in-memory test. The loser gets a plain 409; every other open offer becomes **`WITHDRAWN`, which is deliberately not `EXPIRED`**: a lapsed offer says a worker ignored their phone, a withdrawn one says somebody was quicker, and counting the second against an acceptance rate punishes people for losing races nobody told them they were in. Waves (1 → 3 → 7 km, 15s apart, five per ring, 30s offers) run on a **poller that recomputes from the row**, like delivery's fulfilment job, and every one of those numbers is a config-registry row rather than a deploy. That same `next_wave_at` is all of SPECS §3's "approaching readiness": a courier search that must wait for the kitchen is a job whose first wave is in the future.

  **Results come back as events** (`DispatchAssigned`, `DispatchExhausted` — the latter carrying how many people *saw* it, since zero and fourteen are different failures), never as a call into a vertical by name. `notifications` pushes on `DispatchOffered` — push only, no activity row, because an offer lives thirty seconds and a record of jobs you didn't get is a feed nobody wants. **`partner` now provisions into it** (all three kinds are provisionable; the registration passes a null vehicle category, because approving somebody says they may drive, not what they drive), and **`/v1/me/roles` reads the registry** instead of inferring a driver from an approved application. **Positions arrive over REST, not the WebSocket** — the existing socket has no inbound route, and building one belongs where positions are *watched* (M3), not where they are stored.

  **Verified:** `mvn test` **156/156** (`ModularityTests` green — `partner → dispatch` public-API-only, `notifications → dispatch` events-only — plus 44 new tests: 9 on the geometry, 14 on ranking, 21 on assignment, all pure). **No iOS**: Driver Mode is Phase 3 M2 by the plan, and nothing Swift was touched.

- [x] **Phase 2e · Milestone 5 — Trust & safety baseline** ✅ **deployed** (image `432ecae`, rev 26; `V28` applied and the three Cognito admin actions are on the task role — confirmed against the live account 2026-08-01, having been labelled "not deployed" until then) — the app could publish to strangers and had no way for anybody to get away from anybody. There was no block, no report that did anything, and no way to destroy an account once created. App Store guideline **1.2** makes the first two a condition of shipping user-generated content, and **5.1.1(v)** makes the third one; this milestone is the floor, not a feature.

  **Blocking is one row and two directions.** `social.block` (V28) holds only the blocker's row — blocking is something one person does — but every read filters on "a block exists *either* way, and the filter is the only thing exposed": `BlockService.hiddenFrom` unions the two one-directional queries and nothing else can reach them, because a caller free to use just "who I blocked" writes the bug that makes blocking useless — the person you blocked still reading everything. It **unfollows both ways, severs close-friends and clears mutes in the same transaction as the block**, which is also why the table lives in `social` next to `follow` rather than in `profile`: split across modules, "block also unfollows" becomes eventually consistent, and a safety feature that is briefly wrong is a safety feature that failed. Enforced in the feed (both the followed and the discovery query), post and comment reads and writes, profile views and grids, Watch's feeds and comments, and the marketplace grid — a blocked seller's sofa leaving your grid is less surprising than it staying.

  **Only one half of a block is ever disclosed.** "I blocked them" is a field on the profile view, because that screen is the only place to undo it. "*They* blocked me" is a **404 on the profile**, with the same wording as a profile that never existed, and there is no field anywhere a client could read to infer it. Telling somebody they were blocked hands them a reason to open a second account — and the same reasoning shapes the refusal `messaging` gives, which is one sentence that works in both directions.

  **`messaging` does not read blocking, and that is the design.** Messaging is a platform module; a platform module asking a vertical for permission inverts the layering the whole architecture rests on. So the rule is handed *down*: `messaging` defines a public **`ConversationGuard`**, `social` implements it, and `MessagingService` refuses if any guard objects — checked *before* the reuse path, because reaching an existing thread is opening one. A messaging module with no guards on the classpath (the extracted-service case) behaves exactly as it did before. Existing threads **freeze rather than vanish**: a chat history is a record of something that happened.

  New platform **`moderation`** module (`moderation` schema) — one queue, because a moderator should not have to know that a reported photo and a reported listing were filed in different places. **It stores a pointer and never the content**: everything about the reported thing comes from the vertical that owns its kind, through **`ModeratableContent`**, which `social` (post/comment/story/profile), `watch` (video) and `economy` (listing) implement — the same plugin shape as `ConversationGuard`, same direction. **Reportable kinds are exactly the ones with a handler**; SPECS also lists `MESSAGE`/`ORDER`/`RIDE` and those are deliberately absent, because a report whose content a moderator cannot be shown is a queue item nobody can ever close. **Hide before remove**: a hide is a timestamp, reversible, and its author keeps seeing their own post flagged, so a moderator working at speed can be wrong without being permanent; `REMOVE` is the door that only opens outward. **One decision closes every open report on the same target** (forty rows for one viral post is a queue nobody can work) while **a dismissal closes only its own** (somebody else's complaint about the same post may be a different complaint). An action a vertical cannot perform — hiding a profile is a shadowban, deleting one is its owner's right — throws and becomes a **409 naming the combination**, never a success that did nothing. `SUSPEND` acts on the account rather than the thing, and **is refused by name for a business profile**, whose owner it would otherwise lock out sideways; the refusal prints the owner's id so the moderator makes that call deliberately. Nothing auto-approves, nothing is scheduled, and no report count triggers anything — the same posture partner review takes.

  **Account deletion is a clock, not a button.** `DELETE /v1/me` disables Cognito sign-in *and* revokes the refresh tokens (without the second, the token already on the device keeps minting access tokens for the pool's whole refresh window, and a "closed" account keeps working), then starts a 30-day grace a nightly sweep ends. **Disabling happens first and a failure records nothing** — an account the user has been told is closed and which somebody can still sign into is the one outcome that must not be possible. The scrub keeps the row and destroys the person: handle, name, email, avatar, bio, birth year, interests and the Cognito subject all go, which turns every old post into "Deleted user" **for free**, because every author summary in this codebase already renders a missing profile that way — no per-vertical cascade, and no holes in other people's threads. Clearing the subject is also what lets the same email sign up again later into a fresh, empty profile. Ledger rows are untouched: a financial record is not ours to erase. **Two gaps stated rather than discovered:** cancelling inside the grace cannot be self-service (there is no session left to do it from — an operator restores it from the admin surface, which is what a support request would do anyway), and a business profile owned by a deleted person survives and becomes unmanageable, because winding down a merchant has orders and money in it.

  **Two boundary corrections, both forced and both right.** `auth` gained **`PlatformAdminApi`** — the single definition of "is this caller a platform operator", which `partner` had been keeping a private copy of since 2b M6, and two definitions of that is one definition away from a surface that stays open after the other is locked — and **`AccountAdminApi`** (Cognito disable/enable). `profile` and `moderation` now depend on `auth`, which made `auth`'s own dependency on `profile` a cycle `ModularityTests` refused. **`POST /v1/auth/session` moved into `profile`, at the same URL**: the path says auth and the code says profile, deliberately, and moving the controller rather than the URL is what makes it a compile-time change and not a client one. The property prefix stayed `gojogo.partner.admin` on purpose — renaming it would silently turn partner review off in production on the next deploy.

  **iOS**: a report sheet (reason list from the server, optional note, one Submit — never a one-tap menu item, and it does **not** also hide the thing, because reporting is not a takedown and pretending otherwise teaches people that it is) reachable from a post, a comment, a story, a video, a short, a listing and a profile; **Block** with a confirmation that says what it costs, on a profile and on any post in the feed; **Blocked accounts** and **Delete account** in a new Settings *Safety* group, the latter behind a typed `DELETE` and a screen that explains the thirty days, the sign-out, the tombstone and the kept receipts. "Not interested" stays local and silent beside every Report — a preference is not a complaint and should never queue work for a human. The three presenters that can own these sheets (root, profile, comments) use the same one-owner gating this codebase has paid for before.

  **Verified:** `mvn test` **112/112** (`ModularityTests` green — including the new `social`/`watch`/`economy` → `moderation` and `social` → `messaging` plugin edges — plus 35 new tests: 11 pinning what a block tears down, that it is idempotent, that unblocking does *not* re-follow, that the list is one-directional and that the conversation guard's refusal names no direction; 17 over the moderation decisions, mostly refusals — an unhandled kind named, a self-report, a suspension of a business naming its owner, an identity provider that said no leaving the report **open** rather than recorded as handled, duplicate handlers failing at startup; and 7 on deletion, including that a failed Cognito call records nothing and that asking twice does not buy another thirty days). iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**. Two JPQL constructs were deliberately rewritten into their boring form (`case when` projection → two queries unioned in Java, `count > 0` → a count) because nothing on this machine can execute JPQL and that is exactly the class of defect that first runs in production. **Deploy needs `cdk deploy` as well as an image**: the task role gains `AdminDisableUser` / `AdminEnableUser` / `AdminUserGlobalSignOut`, and without them a suspension and a deletion both refuse with a 503 rather than silently half-working.

- [x] **Tagging + comment replies** ✅ **deployed** (image `432ecae`; `V27` applied — confirmed live 2026-08-01, having been labelled "not deployed" until then) — `@handle` tagging in post captions and in comments, and replying to a comment.

  **Tags are rows, not re-parsed text** (`social.mention`, `V27__mentions_and_comment_replies.sql`). The cheap version leaves the `@amina` in the body and re-resolves it on every render; this app lets people change their handle (V7), so that link quietly breaks the day they do, and the notification fan-out would have to re-parse the text anyway. Resolving once at write time makes "tagged" a fact about the post rather than a guess about its spelling. Each row stores both the `profileId` (the link, rename-proof) and the **handle as written** (the span the client underlines) — the second is history and is never rewritten. One table for both surfaces, keyed `(target_kind, target_id)`, because a caption tag and a comment tag differ only in what they hang off. **A handle that belongs to nobody is not an error**: people type "@" for emphasis and misspell names, so unresolvable tokens are simply not mentions.

  **Replies are one level deep, on purpose.** `social.comment` gains `parent_id` + a denormalised `reply_count`; `parent_id` always points at a *top-level* comment, and a reply to a reply is re-pointed at their shared parent by the service. Instagram, Threads and YouTube all flatten at depth 1 because a phone-width column cannot render a tree — who you are answering is carried by the `@handle` in the reply's own text, which is exactly what the new tagging is for. `GET /v1/posts/{id}/comments` returns the spine with replies nested (two queries however deep the conversation goes), plus `GET /v1/comments/{id}/replies` for a thread the payload only previewed.

  **Notifications route by specificity**: a reply notifies the *comment's* author (`reply`), not the post's — sending both would ping a busy thread's post author dozens of times — and the social module suppresses the "mentioned you" for anyone the same write is already telling by a more specific route, so tagging the person you're replying to is one notification, not two.

  **"Where it's filed" and "who it's addressed to" are different questions**, and conflating them was a real bug caught on review: the flattening resolved a reply-to-a-reply up to the thread root and the *same variable* was then used as the notification's recipient, so answering B's reply inside A's thread told **A** that someone "replied to your comment" and told **B — the person actually being answered — nothing**. The row is still filed under the root (rendering needs one level); the notification now goes to the comment that was tapped, at whatever depth it sits. `CommentReplyAddressingTests` pins the pair apart, and was verified to fail against the old behaviour before being kept. New events `UserMentioned` / `CommentReplied`; `ActivityItem` now carries the `postId` the server always sent, so a tag opens *the post you were tagged in* instead of the old "open my most recent post" guess.

  **The two parsers are the risk, and they are pinned.** The client re-implements the grammar so it can underline tags without asking the server which spans they occupy — a token drawn as tappable that the server never resolved is a dead link, and the reverse is a notification with no visible cause. `MentionParsingTests` fixes the grammar (an email address is not a tag; a trailing dot is punctuation, since handles may contain dots; one person tagged three times is one notification) and the Swift `MentionParser` was **run against those same 20 cases** and agrees on all of them. Change one, change the other.

  **iOS**: tags render semibold-and-tappable rather than in a highlight colour — the palette is monochrome by design, so weight is the emphasis this design system actually has. A shared `MentionAutocomplete` drives an `@`-picker above both composers, backed by the new `GET /v1/profiles/search` and falling back to whoever is already on screen (which is also the whole source in sample mode). The comments sheet gains per-comment **Reply**, indented reply rows, "View N replies", and a "Replying to @x" chip. Video comments stay flat — the `watch` module has no reply table — but their `@handle`s still highlight and link, because the renderer falls back to the handle when no server mention exists. Post/video comment counts now include replies.

  **Forward/backward compatible both ways**: every new field decodes as optional, so a build either side of the roll still renders the feed; against today's prod backend the app posts a reply as a plain comment and shows no server tags, rather than failing.

- [x] **Phase 2e · Milestone 4 — Storefront config (the Studio contract)** ✅ **deployed + prod-E2E-verified (2026-07-31, 46/46)** — every business in this app has looked identical since the day it was provisioned: a hero photo, then the menu, arranged by whoever wrote the SwiftUI. The vision's Studio lets a business arrange its *own* page, and this is the contract it writes through.

  New platform **`storefront` module** (`storefront` schema, `V26__storefront.sql`) owning one thing: a **versioned block document** (`{version, blocks}`) from the **closed set** SPECS §9 fixes — `hero`, `featured_items`, `collection`, `promo_banner`, `media_row`, `text`, `info`. A module rather than a `storefront_json` column on `delivery.merchant`, because a restaurant page, a shop page and a provider's page are the same document with different blocks allowed on it, and three verticals each inventing their own JSON is three renderers that disagree by the second release. **The set is closed per surface, not globally**: `MERCHANT_STOREFRONT` takes all seven, `BUSINESS_HOME` takes only the four that mean anything without a catalog — a menu `collection` on a brand's home page names dishes nobody can resolve, so the write refuses it instead of storing something no renderer will ever draw.

  **The validation split is the design.** The platform module validates *shape* — known type, allowed on this surface, required properties, lengths, block cap, reference kinds, and (deliberately strict) **no property a block's type has no meaning for**, so a hero carrying a `body` is a 400 rather than text that saves and never appears. It cannot validate that item `7f3…` is on *this* menu, so a write takes a `StorefrontReferenceCheck` from the calling vertical **in its signature** — there is no save that forgets to run it. Delivery's checks ITEM / SECTION / PROMOTION against the merchant's own rows and refuses the document whole, naming the id; POST / VIDEO are consciously *not* checked, because delivery has no dependency on `social` or `watch` and an imported post renders through the same public read as anywhere else. Every message names the offending block by its author-chosen id, because a page builder's error banner has to be able to highlight one block. **Reads are the opposite of strict**: a document that won't parse comes back empty and logs, since the decorative half of a commerce page must never take the functional half down with it.

  Wired into both surfaces the plan names: owner-scoped **`GET|PUT /v1/delivery/merchants/mine/storefront`** (public read embedded in `GET /v1/delivery/merchants/{id}`, detail only, like the menu) and **`GET|PUT /v1/profiles/businesses/{id}/home`** (public read embedded in the profile view `social` builds — a business *is* a profile, so that is where one is rendered from). Written **whole**, not block-by-block: a page is an ordered list, and a patch API over an ordered list invents a reordering protocol nobody asked for. **iOS never edits it** — this is GoJoAdmin's write API — but it now *renders* it: hero (with a button that actually scrolls to the dish or section it names), item rails resolved against the menu already on screen, a `promo_banner` that draws the **live** promotion rather than copy that could drift from it, prose, and an info strip read off live merchant data. Unknown block types — and `media_row`, whose posts and videos need reads the delivery screen has no business making — are **skipped silently**, which is the forward-compat contract that lets the server start accepting a new type the day it ships.

  **Verified:** `mvn test` **64/64** (`ModularityTests` green — `delivery`/`profile`/`social` → `storefront` are all public-API-only — plus 26 new tests over the write contract: every type accepted on a storefront, an unknown type refused *by name*, catalog blocks refused on a business home, both halves of a button required together, a media row refusing an ITEM, duplicate block ids, the block and rail caps, repeated items collapsing, empty strings not counting as values, and reference extraction finding ids in rails, in a hero's button and in a media row). iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**. **Deployed** as task definition rev 23 (image `6634d41`), health 200 through the ALB — which is a real database round-trip, so `V26` migrated cleanly. **Prod E2E 46/46**: a seven-block page written and read back in the author's order, embedded in the customer's restaurant detail and absent from browse rows, every refusal refusing (unknown type named, a hero's `body`, a duplicate id, a button with nowhere to go, 25 blocks) and **none of them touching the stored page or its version**, a second merchant's owner refused when featuring the first's *real* dish — the cross-tenant case, not a made-up UUID — a business home taking content blocks and refusing catalog ones, a person's profile carrying an empty page rather than a null, and clearing a page still counting as a save. **Simulator pass on the live backend**: a page arranged by curl on a real prod restaurant rendered hero → featured rail → promo banner → about → info strip → menu, in the author's order, with `media_row` correctly absent; the rail's + put the dish in the same cart as the menu row (`$23.99`), the banner's *Use* applied `WELCOME10` and became *Applied*, and the hero's *See the menu* scrolled to the section it names. See the Phase 2e M4 section below.
- [x] **Phase 2e · Milestone 3 — Payments + the GoJo Wallet** ✅ **deployed + prod-E2E-verified (2026-07-31)** — every commerce surface built so far stopped at the moment somebody owed something. A delivery order was priced by the server and then placed with **no payment step at all**; a listing conversation had nowhere to become a sale; the merchant an approval creates was a payee with no way to be paid. This is the ledger all of that was waiting for.

  New platform **`payments` module** (`payments` schema, `V23__payments.sql`). One **double-entry ledger**, not per-vertical balances — a vertical keeping its own would eventually disagree with this one, and reconciling two ledgers is a job nobody volunteers for. Accounts are `(owner kind, owner id, bucket)`: `USER` / `MERCHANT` / `PLATFORM` / `EXTERNAL` across `AVAILABLE` / `ESCROW` / `STAKING` / `TOKENS` / `REWARDS`, which is Phase 3's staking and ride tokens already having somewhere to live. Balances are **materialised** because a checkout reads them, and `LedgerAuditJob` re-derives every one of them nightly rather than trusting the shortcut. Every write takes a caller-chosen **idempotency key that is UNIQUE in the database**, so a retried checkout, a replayed webhook and a double-tapped button collapse onto one movement and the second caller is told so rather than lied to. Accounts are locked in a fixed global order — a settlement touches four rows, and consistent ordering is the difference between a busy Friday and a deadlock storm.

  **Stripe is the boundary, not the book.** Money in is a **hosted Checkout session** (the card never touches this backend or the app, so the PCI surface is zero and there is no card data in this system to leak); money out is a **Connect transfer**. Both are mirrored as entries against `EXTERNAL`, whose balance negated is what the platform has taken in net of payouts. **The wallet is credited by the signed webhook, never by the browser coming back** — a client reaching a success URL proves a browser reached a URL. With no Stripe keys the ledger still holds, captures, releases and transfers every cent (*unconfigured is a mode, not a failure*), and with no webhook secret a top-up is **refused rather than started**, because a payment this backend can take but cannot learn about is a customer charged for nothing.

  **Delivery checkout** (`V24`) is the first consumer. Placement holds the total in the customer's **own** `ESCROW` — cancelling is therefore a *release*, not a refund; delivery splits it: food minus commission to the merchant, commission plus service fee to the platform, and the delivery fee and tip **under their own ledger kinds** to the courier Phase 4 will create (folding them into commission would have made a year of tips indistinguishable from platform revenue). **Tips are 100% the courier's**; **promotions** are a real table (codes, caps, per-user limits, windows) applied server-side at pricing time, and the discount always comes off the *merchant's* side — a merchant funds their own campaign, and no promotion may reduce what the courier is paid. New **`POST /v1/delivery/orders/quote`** prices a basket before anything is charged, so the app offers a top-up instead of walking someone into a 402. Also here: the **platform config registry** (`V22`, SPECS §14 — effective-dated policy knobs behind `ConfigApi`, so a fee change stops being a deploy) and the **`OrderStatusChanged` consumer** the event had been publishing into the void for since 2b M4.

  **Verified:** `mvn test` **38/38** (`ModularityTests` green — `delivery → payments`/`config` is public-API-only, `notifications → payments` is events-only — plus 25 new tests over the arithmetic and the signature check: basket totals summing exactly to what each payee gets, a discount never exceeding the food, free delivery still paying the courier in full, fee rounding always favouring the payee, and a Stripe webhook refused when tampered, when stale, when wrongly signed, and when unconfigured). iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**. **Prod E2E green**: 13/13 on the wallet (a real Checkout session minted against `acct_1SxSM6GkYdm9mdqz`, a signed `checkout.session.completed` crediting the wallet, a replay changing nothing, an unsigned and a tampered delivery both 401) and 30+ on checkout (quote → hold → the fulfilment timeline → capture split → merchant balance `3060` = (4000 − 400) − 15%, escrow empty, a post-delivery tip, cancel → release → money back, payout refused before onboarding with a reason). **Three of the E2E's assertions were the script's own bugs** and were re-verified individually (6/6): an unknown code is **404**, an empty wallet is **402** carrying the shortfall, and the restaurant's flag is `open`. Simulator pass on the live backend: the wallet renders the real ledger and a tip tap re-quoted $23.99 → $25.99 through production. **Deploying it took four attempts and that is the story worth keeping** — see the incidents log, and the new `schema-check` CI workflow that would have caught two of them. See the Phase 2e M3 section below.
- [x] **Sumsub KYC — identity verification, for real** ✅ **deployed + configured in production (2026-07-30)** — the platform had two ways to prove who someone is, and both were bad. In the app, the driver/courier flow asked for a name, a document number, and two tiles you tapped to *claim* you'd photographed your ID; nothing checked any of it. On the merchant side, a real ID card was uploaded into a private S3 prefix for a human to squint at — which works, but means GojoGo accumulates copies of everyone's passport, and a human comparing a face to a photo is not a liveness check. Both are now replaced by the vendor SPECS §4 always intended (impl #2 of `IdentityVerificationApi`, which is the only reason this was a config swap and not a rewrite).

  New platform **`kyc` module** (`kyc` schema, `V21__kyc.sql`) — its own module rather than a corner of `partner` or `auth`, because driver staking (Phase 3), sellers and providers (Phase 5) and payouts all need a verified person and none of them should own the check (ARCHITECTURE §2b decision 3). **The module stores a verdict, not evidence**: status, the vendor's client-facing reason, reject labels, and the applicant id needed to ask again. The documents, the selfie and the extracted personal data stay with Sumsub, which is the entire point of using them.

  **Two ways a verdict arrives, and both are load-bearing.** The fast path is a webhook (`POST /v1/kyc/webhook`), authenticated by HMAC over the **raw** request body — so the handler takes `byte[]`, since re-serialising the JSON would break every legitimate delivery — and idempotent on the delivery id, because Sumsub retries anything non-2xx and a repeated `applicantReviewed` would re-fire the event that flips a partner's badge and wakes their phone. The fallback is a pull (`POST /v1/kyc/refresh`) the app runs the moment the SDK closes. That fallback is not a nicety: a webhook needs a public URL configured in someone else's console, and a KYC flow that is undeliverable until then is a KYC flow that blocks onboarding. **With no webhook secret set the endpoint refuses everything (503)** — an unverifiable payload must never be able to mark someone VERIFIED.

  **iOS** gets the native **IdensicMobileSDK** (SPM, 1.45.1) rather than a web view: the camera capture and liveness are the product here. `IdentityVerificationFlow` is the one place in the app that touches UIKit — it mints a token, wires the SDK's expiration handler (tokens are short-lived on purpose, so a slow verification dies without it), and suspends until dismissal. It deliberately **discards the SDK's own verdict** and re-asks our server; the client's impression of a decision the server owns is not something a submission should be gated on. The partner flow's identity card is now a rendering of a status, with the vendor's own wording on a refusal and a "check again" for a pending one.

  **The gate is at submission, not approval** — a reviewer's queue should never contain an application that cannot be approved, and the applicant finds out while they can still act. It applies to the **admin-side create** too, which is the one workflow change: an operator can type a merchant's address and upload their licence, but nobody passes a liveness check for somebody else. With the vendor on, the identity document kinds drop out of `requiredDocuments` (collecting an ID we've chosen not to store would be theatre), leaving `RESTAURANT` needing only its licence and `DRIVER`/`COURIER` needing no uploads at all. **`partner`'s human approval is unchanged**: Sumsub answers "is this person who they say they are"; whether a *business* should trade here is still a person's decision.

  **Verified:** `mvn test` **10/10** (`ModularityTests` green — `partner → kyc` is public-API-only — plus 9 new tests over the vendor→platform translation: GREEN→VERIFIED, RED+RETRY→RESUBMISSION_REQUESTED vs RED+FINAL→REJECTED, an unknown `reviewStatus` never reading as a verdict, out-of-order deliveries not un-verifying someone, and a repeated verdict reporting "no change"). iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED** with the SDK linked. The sandbox credentials and the exact request-signing scheme were confirmed live against `api.sumsub.com`: access-token mint **200**, applicant pull **404** on an unknown user (which is what the client treats as "never started"). **Deployed:** merged as PR #1 (`1d14b48`), which CI auto-shipped as task definition rev 15 — code live but *unconfigured*, because CI copies env/secrets from the previous revision verbatim and never runs CDK. `cdk deploy` then added the three `SUMSUB_*` secrets as rev 16. The half-state is worth remembering: `/v1/kyc/webhook` answered **503** with the code live and no credentials, which looks like a working endpoint refusing you rather than a missing one. **Verified in prod:** startup log reads `Sumsub KYC on: production environment, level 'id-and-liveness', webhooks verified`, and the webhook went 503 → **401** on an unsigned payload — the signature check refusing a forged delivery, which is the behaviour that matters. **The webhook path is proven end to end against production** — Sumsub's own *Test webhook* button can't do it (it builds its sample from a real applicant in a matching status, and the account has none, so it errors with "You don't have any applicants in the following statuses: completed"). Proven instead by three signed deliveries to the live endpoint: a correctly-signed `applicantReviewed` → **200**, reaching `KycService` and dropping as an unknown applicant (the guard that made the probe stateless); a tampered digest → **401**; and a valid digest with the body edited `GREEN`→`RED` after signing → **401**, which is what proves the digest covers the raw bytes rather than a re-serialisation. Still unexercised: a real applicant end to end. Sandbox has no approval algorithms and this went straight to production credentials, so the first genuine verification is also the first full run of the SDK → token-refresh → webhook → verdict → partner-gate loop.

- [x] **Phase 2e · Milestone 2 — Real admin principal + the admin-side create** ✅ **deployed + prod-E2E-verified (2026-07-30, 24/24)** — the review surface had exactly one way in (a shared secret with no identity) and no way to *file* an application at all, because every applicant endpoint is scoped to its caller while restaurants are created in the admin tool. Both fixed. A **`platform-admin` Cognito group** (added to `GojoGoAuthStack`) arrives in the reviewer's own ID token as `cognito:groups`, so a KYC decision is now attributable to a person — the approve/reject/suspend log lines name them — and `PARTNER_ADMIN_TOKEN` drops to **break-glass** rather than being the only key (the queue exists without it now). The **admin-side create**: `POST /v1/partner/admin/applications` names the owner by profile id or `@handle` and then reuses the merchant's *own* write path — one set of rules, not an admin mirror — plus admin `submit`, the three document verbs (presigned into the **applicant's** private prefix, never the operator's), a per-document freshly-signed link for a console left open past the 10-minute expiry, and a `kind` filter on the queue. `GET /v1/me/roles` gains `isPlatformAdmin`, read from the claim. A **CORS allowlist** (`WEB_ALLOWED_ORIGINS`, empty by default = no headers, exactly as today) means GoJoAdmin's browser origin is config, not a code change. **The E2E found a bug that had been live since M6:** the task role could *sign* a GET for a KYC document but had no `s3:GetObject` on the private prefix, so every reviewer link 403'd — a presigned URL carries the signer's permissions (see incidents).
