The interval is told by the server (`positionIntervalSeconds`), because the cost of it is the server's problem.

**API** (worker-facing only — a rider never talks to dispatch; `travel` asks on their behalf):

| Verb | Path | |
|---|---|---|
| `GET` | `/v1/dispatch/me` | registrations + open offers + current assignment, one call. Empty lists for somebody never approved, **not a 404** — "am I a driver" is a question the app asks before it knows |
| `POST` | `/v1/dispatch/me/availability` | `{kind, available}`; refused by name while suspended, ignored while `BUSY` (abandoning somebody on a pavement is a cancellation, not a switch) |
| `POST` | `/v1/dispatch/me/position` | 204 |
| `POST` | `/v1/dispatch/offers/{id}/accept` | the assignment, or a 409 saying who got there first |
| `POST` | `/v1/dispatch/offers/{id}/decline` | 204; declining twice is not an error |

**No iOS in this milestone**, by the plan: Driver Mode — availability toggle, offers, earnings, performance — is Phase 3 M2, alongside vehicles and the stake. Nothing Swift was touched, and `/v1/me/roles` kept its shape (the app decodes `isDriver`/`isCourier` optionally and doesn't branch on them yet).

**Verified:** `mvn test` **156/156** on Corretto 21 — `ModularityTests` green, including the new `partner → dispatch` (public API only) and `notifications → dispatch` (events only) edges — plus **44 new tests**, all pure. 9 on the geometry (a degree of longitude shrinking towards the poles, which is the error flat arithmetic would make; the box covering the ring at every bearing on five radii; polar and antimeridian windows opening rather than narrowing wrongly). 14 on ranking (stale positions skipped even when nearest, no position at all, the wrong vehicle, already-asked excluded, the rating floor at its boundary, idle time winning and losing, idle credit saturating, the wave truncating best-first, and the same wave ranking identically when the rows arrive in a different order). 21 on assignment (the second accept, the optimistic-lock race publishing nothing, a lapsed accept counting as ignored, someone else's offer as a 404, withdrawn-not-expired, dual-mode busy and dual-mode position fan-out, idempotent request and decline, cancellation counting only when the caller says it was the worker's fault, and a stored vehicle category this build doesn't know being skipped rather than thrown on).

**Deployed** 2026-08-01 as task definition **rev 27** / image `b3901f6`, alongside M2. `V29` applied cleanly at startup and the app came up with no warnings; `/v1/dispatch/me` answers 401 unauthenticated, as every JWT surface does. ~~**No prod E2E yet**~~ — **run 2026-08-01** with M4 (see that section). An approved driver signed on, reported a position, and dispatch offered a real ride within ~4 seconds; both answers were exercised — accepting in dispatch at the asking price, and declining to counter in `travel`. The wave also ran the new `WorkerEligibility` veto for the first time. That run is what found the M3 transaction defect in the incident log: the accept-in-dispatch path had **never once executed**, and it did not work.

**What this does not do, and the next milestone does.** No vehicles, no `$30` stake, no KYC fee (Phase 3 M2 — the stake is a `WalletApi` hold into `STAKING`, which 2e M3 already built the bucket for). No ride: `travel` doesn't exist, so `RIDE` is a `JobKind` nothing yet publishes (M3). Delivery still runs `OrderFulfilmentJob`'s simulated courier — swapping it for a real one is Phase 4 M1, and the swap 2b M4 was explicitly designed for. Routing and ETAs are straight-line distance; SPECS §3's Mapbox Directions authority arrives with the live trip that needs a polyline. And no negotiation — an accept is an accept, because fare counter-offers are `travel` order state (SPECS §2) and layer *on top* of an offer rather than inside it.

## Phase 2e · Milestone 4 — Storefront config, the Studio contract (deployed + prod-E2E-verified 2026-07-31)

**What was missing.** Every business in this app looks the same, because the app decides what a business looks like: a hero photo, a stats line, then the menu. The vision's Studio is a business arranging its own page — a banner, the dishes it wants seen first, a collection, its current offer, its own words. This milestone is the document that arrangement lives in, and the API GoJoAdmin will write it through (ARCHITECTURE §10b).

**One module, one document, two surfaces.** New platform `storefront` module ([package-info](backend/src/main/java/com/gojogo/storefront/package-info.java), `V26__storefront.sql`). A page is `{version, blocks}`; a block is one of a **closed set** (SPECS §9) and nothing else. The alternative — a `storefront_json` column on `delivery.merchant` — was fewer files today and three divergent renderers by Phase 5, since `economy` and `services` get storefronts on this same contract.

The set is closed **per surface**, which is the part worth keeping:

| Surface | Owner | Blocks |
|---|---|---|
| `MERCHANT_STOREFRONT` | a vertical's merchant (`delivery` today) | all seven |
| `BUSINESS_HOME` | a business profile (2e M1) | `hero`, `media_row`, `text`, `info` |

A menu `collection` on a brand's home page points at dishes nobody can resolve; a `promo_banner` there names a promotion table the profile module doesn't have. Refusing them at the write is what keeps the renderer free of "this block is only sometimes meaningful" branches.

**Who validates what.** The split runs along what each side can actually know:

- **`storefront` validates shape.** Known type (an unknown one is a 400 *naming it*, which is why `type` stays a string until validation resolves it — a Jackson type-id resolver turns the same mistake into a parse failure several layers below anything that could say which block). Allowed on this surface. Required properties, lengths, the block cap and the per-block ref cap (both `platform.config` knobs, so a page-size decision stops being a deploy). Reference *kinds* — a button goes to an ITEM or a SECTION, a media row holds POSTs and VIDEOs. And, deliberately strict, **no property a block's type has no meaning for**: a hero carrying a `body` is refused, because the alternative is a builder that sends it, an author who sees their text in the preview and never on the page, and a bug report about "text that doesn't save".
- **The vertical validates the ids.** Only `delivery` can say whether item `7f3…` is on *this* menu, so `save(...)` takes a `StorefrontReferenceCheck` **as a parameter** rather than as a convention — there is no save that skips it. Delivery checks ITEM / SECTION / PROMOTION and refuses the document **whole**, naming the id, rather than storing it with the bad block dropped: silently editing what an author wrote is worse than telling them no. POST / VIDEO are consciously unchecked — delivery has no dependency on `social` or `watch` and shouldn't grow one to police decoration, and an imported post renders through the same public read as everywhere else, so a borrowed id shows something that was already public.

**Reads are forgiving, writes are strict**, and that asymmetry is the whole availability story: a document written by a newer deploy that this one can't parse comes back as an *empty page* with a log line, because a restaurant losing its header is a smaller outage than a restaurant losing its menu. Same reasoning behind `StorefrontDocument.empty()` being a document rather than a null — the merchant payload always carries a storefront, so an un-arranged restaurant renders exactly the screen it rendered before this milestone.

**Storage.** One row per (surface, owner): a generated id with the pair as a unique index, *not* an assigned composite key — an entity with one makes every JPA save a merge, and "arrange for the first time" and "rewrite" stop being distinguishable operations. `blocks` is JSON in a `text` column rather than `jsonb`: nothing queries inside a document, so jsonb would buy only a Hibernate binding this project cannot test locally (see "No local database"), and the validator is a stronger shape guarantee than "it parses" anyway. A JPA `@Version` column, because two page builders saving at once is exactly the case this module exists to serve and the loser should be told.

**iOS renders it, and only renders it.** No editor — that is Studio's job, and `merchantDashboardEnabled` still gates the menu editor pending the same call. The restaurant screen now draws, above the menu: a **hero** whose button genuinely scrolls to the dish or section it names (a `ScrollViewReader` over ids that are the *server's*, which is what lets a block authored in a console point into this list); **item rails** resolved against the menu already on screen, so a sold-out featured dish is simply absent rather than a card that 400s at checkout; a **promo banner** that renders the *live* promotion the block names by id — the first time the promotions built in 2e M3 are visible in the app — and nothing at all once it ends; **prose**; and an **info** strip read off live merchant data, which is why an info block is forbidden from carrying its own copy of the delivery fee. That strip shows ETA, fee and rating today; SPECS §9 specifies it as "hours/delivery/**pickup**", and **pickup does not exist** — a gap this milestone surfaced and did not create (the vision and the whole phase plan assumed it too). Written up as the first item of Phase 4 M4; the strip gains a column the day it lands, with no change to the document contract, because an info block holds nothing. Unknown types are **skipped silently** — the forward-compat contract that lets the server accept a new block type the day it ships — and `media_row` skips with them for now, since its posts and videos need reads the delivery screen has no business making. That is the one block the server accepts and iOS does not yet draw.

**Verified:** `mvn test` **64/64** on Corretto 21. `ModularityTests` green — `delivery`, `profile` and `social` each reach `storefront` through its public API only. 26 new tests, all pure: the closed set per surface, an unknown type refused by name, a button needing both a label and a destination, a media row refusing an ITEM, duplicate block ids, both caps, repeated ids collapsing, blank strings not counting as values, and reference extraction finding ids in rails, in a hero's button and in a media row separately by kind. iOS **BUILD SUCCEEDED**.

**Deployed** 2026-07-31 — `./scripts/deploy-backend.sh` shipped image `6634d41` as task definition **rev 23**; no `--infra` needed, since this milestone adds no secret and no stack change. Health 200 through the ALB on the full check, which round-trips the database, so `V26` applied cleanly. First deploy of the four-attempt era to go in on one attempt.

**Prod E2E — 46/46** (`verify_storefront.sh`, session-local, two real Cognito users against `https://api.gojogo.app`):

- a seven-block page written and read back **in the author's order**, the hero keeping its button, and an `info` block coming back carrying nothing but `id` and `type`
- the public read **embedded** in the customer's `GET /v1/delivery/merchants/{id}` at the author's version, and **absent from browse rows** — a query per row to draw a card nobody arranged is exactly what the detail-only rule avoids
- every refusal refused: an unknown type (message names `carousel_3d`), a hero carrying `body`, a foreign dish and a foreign promotion (both named), two blocks sharing an id, a button with a label and nowhere to go, and 25 blocks against the config knob — **and none of them changed the stored page or bumped its version**, which is the assertion that proves a refusal is a refusal rather than a partial write
- the cross-tenant case with **real** ids: the second test user's restaurant owner featuring the *first* user's actual dish is a 400 naming it, and the first user's page is untouched. (The first draft asserted "a caller who manages no restaurant gets 404" — both test accounts turned out to own one, so the assumption was the script's bug; the stronger assertion replaced it.)
- the business home page: content blocks accepted, a catalog `collection` refused with "which this page doesn't take", someone else's business **403**, the document embedded in the public profile view, and a **person's** profile carrying an empty page at version 0 rather than a null
- clearing a page with `{"blocks":[]}` is a valid save that still bumps the version

Two of the run's early failures were the script's own (a `$STATUS` set inside a command substitution never reaches the parent shell, and version numbers asserted absolutely against a prod row that had already been written). Both were harness bugs, not backend ones — the content assertions passed on the first run.

**Simulator pass on the live backend.** A page was arranged by curl on a real prod restaurant (hero → featured rail → promo banner → about → info → media_row) and opened in the app as a *customer*: it rendered in the author's order, `media_row` correctly absent, the info strip showing live values (`25 min` / `$3.00` / `4.5`), the rail's **+** putting the dish in the same cart as the menu row below it (`View cart · $23.99`), the banner's **Use** applying `WELCOME10` and flipping to **Applied**, and the hero's **See the menu** scrolling to the section it names. That last one is the whole reason the ids on the menu rows are the server's.

## Phase 2e · Milestone 3 — Payments + the GoJo Wallet (deployed + prod-E2E-verified 2026-07-31)

**The shape of the money.** One double-entry ledger in a new platform `payments` module. Accounts are `(owner kind, owner id, bucket, currency)`; a movement debits one and credits another, so the sum of all balances never changes and money cannot be *created* by a bug — only misplaced, which is findable. `EXTERNAL` is the counterparty for card money and the only account allowed to go negative; its balance negated is what the platform has taken in net of payouts.

```
placement   AVAILABLE ── total ──▶ ESCROW              (still the customer's)
delivered   ESCROW ── food − commission ──▶ merchant
            ESCROW ── commission + service fee ──▶ platform
            ESCROW ── delivery fee ──▶ platform         (the courier's, Phase 4)
            ESCROW ── tip ──▶ platform                  (the courier's, Phase 4)
cancelled   ESCROW ── total ──▶ AVAILABLE
```

The two payees that don't exist yet settle under their **own** ledger kinds (`COURIER_FEE`, `TIP`) rather than being folded into commission. Phase 4 M1 redirects them by changing a payee; had they been recorded as platform revenue, a year of tips would be indistinguishable from it afterwards.

**Why it is built the way it is:**

- **Idempotency is the contract.** Every write takes a caller-chosen key that is `UNIQUE` in the database, derived from the thing being paid for (`order:{id}:hold`), never generated. A retried checkout, a replayed webhook and a double-tapped button collapse onto one movement, and the second caller is told `alreadyApplied` rather than lied to.
- **Balances are materialised, and checked.** A checkout reads a balance, and summing an append-only table for it gets slower forever. `LedgerAuditJob` re-derives every balance from the entries nightly and shouts if one drifted — it corrects nothing, because a drift means a bug or a hand-written UPDATE and both want a human, not an automatic "fix" that erases the evidence.
- **Rows are locked in a fixed global order.** A settlement touches four or five accounts; consistent ordering is what keeps two concurrent settlements queueing instead of deadlocking.
- **Stripe is the boundary.** Hosted Checkout for money in (no card data exists anywhere in this system), Connect transfers for money out, both mirrored as ledger entries. **The wallet is credited by the signed webhook, never by the browser returning** — that is the single most common way a wallet gets funded by a payment that never happened. A payout **debits first and transfers second, in two transactions**: one that paid out without debiting is unrecoverable, one that debited and failed is a FAILED row and a reversing entry.
- **Unconfigured is a mode.** No Stripe keys → the ledger still holds, captures, releases and transfers every cent; only the outside world is unreachable. No webhook secret → a top-up is **refused rather than started**, because a payment we can take but cannot learn about is a customer charged for nothing.
- **The vertical owns its payee's surface.** A merchant's balance and payouts live on `delivery`'s `/mine` endpoints, not on payments': only the module that owns a merchant can prove the caller owns it, and delivery already depends on payments — the reverse would be an immediate cycle. Phase 3's drivers arrive the same way.

**Delivery checkout** (`V24`). `POST /v1/delivery/orders/quote` prices a basket — including whether the wallet covers it — before anything is charged, so the app offers a top-up instead of walking someone into a 402. The order carries `payment_status` (`UNPAID` / `HELD` / `CAPTURED` / `RELEASED` / `REFUNDED`; `UNPAID` is what every order placed before this migration genuinely was, not a null). **Promotions** are a real table — codes, caps, per-user limits, windows — applied server-side at pricing time, because a discount the client computed is a discount the client chose; the discount always comes off the **merchant's** side, free delivery included, so no campaign a restaurant runs can reduce what the courier is paid. All the arithmetic lives in one `Basket` record used by both quoting and placing: two code paths computing the same total is how people get billed for surprises, and settlement reads the same numbers back, which is what guarantees the splits sum to exactly what was held (`OrderPayments.settle` refuses rather than stranding a difference in escrow).

**Platform config registry** (`V22`, SPECS §14). `platform.config` + `ConfigApi`: effective-dated policy values, read with a compiled-in default so an empty table behaves identically to a populated one. Never updated in place — a change inserts a row with a later `effective_from`, which is what makes "what was the fee last March" answerable from the table, the question every payment dispute eventually becomes. No write endpoint yet, deliberately: values are seeded by migration until GoJoAdmin exists to edit them, the same posture the music catalog and partner review took.

**iOS.** A **GoJo Wallet** screen (balance, what's held, a statement that reads like a receipt because a capture writes one line per payee) and a checkout that stops adding anything up — the tip picker and promo field re-quote against the server rather than recompute, and a 402 opens the wallet with the exact shortfall. Top-ups leave the app entirely for a Stripe-hosted page in an `ASWebAuthenticationSession`; coming back is not treated as payment, the app re-asks the server. The merchant dashboard gains an earnings card with Connect onboarding. All of it now reached through a new **Settings** page rather than the profile's overflow menu, which had been growing an entry per milestone.

**Runbook — the Stripe side (done 2026-07-31, repeat for live mode):**

```
# 1 · the webhook FIRST — Stripe mints the signing secret when the endpoint is created
curl https://api.stripe.com/v1/webhook_endpoints -u "$STRIPE_SECRET_KEY:" \
  -d url="https://api.gojogo.app/v1/payments/webhook" \
  -d "enabled_events[]=checkout.session.completed" \
  -d "enabled_events[]=checkout.session.expired" \
  -d "enabled_events[]=checkout.session.async_payment_succeeded" \
  -d "enabled_events[]=checkout.session.async_payment_failed" \
  -d "enabled_events[]=account.updated"
# 2 · put all three keys in one secret, then deploy the stack that reads it
aws secretsmanager put-secret-value --secret-id gojogo/stripe --secret-string \
  '{"secretKey":"sk_…","webhookSecret":"whsec_…","publishableKey":"pk_…"}'
./scripts/deploy-backend.sh --infra     # carries every -c …SecretArn; see the script header
```

Test mode and live mode are **different endpoints and different signing secrets**; the key prefix (`sk_test_`) is the only visible difference, which is why the startup log says which one it came up in: `Stripe on: TEST (no real money moves) mode, webhooks verified`.

## Phase 2e · Milestone 2 — Real admin principal + the admin-side create (deployed + verified 2026-07-30)

Everything GoJoAdmin needs on its day one has to exist as an API before it is built (ARCHITECTURE §10b). Two things didn't.

**An operator was not a person.** Review sat outside the JWT chain behind one shared secret, because when it was built this app had no admin role. Now it does: a **`platform-admin` Cognito group** ([auth-stack.ts](infra/lib/auth-stack.ts)), which Cognito puts in the reviewer's own ID token as a `cognito:groups` claim. [PlatformAdmins](backend/src/main/java/com/gojogo/partner/internal/PlatformAdmins.java) resolves a request to an `AdminActor` — a named person from their token, or `break-glass` — and every decision log line now carries it. `PARTNER_ADMIN_TOKEN` is kept deliberately: losing the ability to approve partners takes GojoDelivery's supply side with it, so there has to be a way in when Cognito is the broken thing. It is no longer *required* — with it unset, the queue is group-only, which is the intended end state. A caller who is neither still gets **404**, not 403: these paths don't admit they exist.

Adding an operator is a manual, auditable act, and the group is **empty in prod right now**:

```bash
aws cognito-idp admin-add-user-to-group --user-pool-id us-east-1_ImKOJoJaA \
  --username <your-email> --group-name platform-admin
```

The claim is read per-request, so removing someone revokes them the moment their current token expires — verified: the test account 404'd on the queue immediately after removal.

**Nobody could file an application.** Restaurants are created in Gojo Admin (2026-07-27), but every applicant endpoint is scoped to its caller, so the operator's only route into `partner` was the database. `POST /v1/partner/admin/applications` closes it: name the owner by `ownerProfileId` or `ownerHandle`, and the application is written through **the merchant's own `save`** — one write path, one set of completeness rules, no admin mirror to drift. The rest of the journey follows: admin `submit` (same rules the merchant's own submit runs), the three document verbs presigning into the **applicant's** `private/partner/{userId}/` prefix rather than the operator's, and a per-document freshly-signed link, because a ten-minute expiry is shorter than a careful review of someone's ID.

**Also in this slice:** a `kind` filter on the queue; `isPlatformAdmin` on `/v1/me/roles` (derived from the claim, like every other role); and a **CORS allowlist** — `WEB_ALLOWED_ORIGINS`, empty by default, which emits no CORS headers at all and is exactly today's behaviour. Native apps aren't subject to CORS, so an allowance would exist purely for someone else; GoJoAdmin's origin goes in when it exists (`cdk deploy GojoGoFargateStack -c webAllowedOrigins=https://admin.gojogo.app`).

**What the E2E caught (live since M6):** the reviewer's signed document links had never once been fetched, and they didn't work — the ECS task role could sign a GET but had no `s3:GetObject` on `private/*`, and **a presigned URL carries the signer's permissions**. Every KYC document was unopenable. Fixed with a prefix-scoped `grantRead` on the task role, deployed, and re-verified: the link returns the object.

**iOS: deliberately nothing.** This app has no admin surface and isn't getting one; the operator's client is GoJoAdmin. The only iOS-visible change is a new boolean on a payload it already fetches.

**Verified in prod (2026-07-30, `verify_admin_principal.sh`, session-local, 24/24):** A in the group reads the queue, B (ordinary user) 404s, anonymous 404s → admin files an application for an owner by handle (201), unknown owner 404, B can't file for anyone → the owner sees it as theirs on `/v1/partner/me` → operator presigns + uploads + attaches all three required papers into the applicant's private prefix → fresh signed link 200 **and fetches the bytes** → B can't read the papers → admin submit → SUBMITTED → admin approve → APPROVED, merchant provisioned → the owner's `/v1/delivery/merchants/mine` returns that merchant → suspend, so no test restaurant can take an order. The test account was then removed from the group.

## Phase 2e · Milestone 1 — Business profiles (built 2026-07-30, not deployed)

The commerce spine starts with identity. The vision's journey for every economic player — restaurant, seller, service provider — is *create a business profile, build an audience, then activate commerce*, so the business has to be a real, followable, publishing identity **before** any money exists.

**The decision that shapes everything: a business is a profile.** `V20__business_profiles.sql` adds `kind = PERSON | BUSINESS` to `profile.user_profile`, drops the `NOT NULL` on `cognito_sub` (a business has no sign-in of its own — Postgres allows many NULLs under a UNIQUE constraint, so sign-in uniqueness is untouched), and adds `profile.business_profile`: owner, contact, address + geo, opening hours, `verified`. Fields every profile already has — name, handle, bio, `category`, avatar — are **not** duplicated there; the business half only holds what a person has no use for. The consequence is what the milestone is worth: the social graph, feed, stories, Watch, notifications and the future search index all work on a business the moment it exists, because none of them ever knew anything but profiles. The alternative — a `business` module with its own graph — forks every social feature forever.

**Acting as a business.** Every endpoint that *creates* content takes an optional `actAsProfileId` (`/v1/posts`, `/v1/stories`, `/v1/videos`, both comment paths, `/v1/media/presign`); likes and follows take `?actAs=`. Each resolves through one method — `ProfileApi.requireActingProfile(caller, actAs)` — which returns the caller unless they own the business, and 403s otherwise. No second token, no session switch, nothing the client is trusted about. Reads are deliberately unchanged: `liked` / `bookmarked` / feed are decorated for the caller, which is exactly why **iOS only uses act-as for creation** — a business "like" would come back unliked on the next refresh, and an identity that disagrees with itself is worse than one that can't like.

**The badge means one thing.** `partner.partner_account` gains `business_profile_id` (verified through the same ownership check when it's set). Approving that application sets `verified = true` on the business, suspending it clears it, restoring sets it again — and the owner-scoped edit surface has no path to it at all. Verification is the KYC review's to grant; it is never bought (SPECS §8).

**Roles are derived, not stored.** `GET /v1/me/roles` answers "what can this account be right now" from the facts themselves: businesses owned (`profile`), applications and provisioned merchants (`partner`), driver/courier (an approved application today, the dispatch registry from Phase 3). There is no role table to drift out of sync. It lives in `partner` because `partner → profile` is an allowed direction and the reverse would be a Modulith cycle.

**iOS.**

| Surface | What it does |
|---|---|
| **Business profiles sheet** ([BusinessProfilesView](GojoGo/Screens/BusinessProfilesView.swift)) | Off your own profile — the handle chevron, or the menu. Lists "you" plus every business, one tap switches who you publish as, and the row menu opens the page or the editor. Create/edit is one form (name, username, category, about, contact, address, hours, logo) |
| **[ActingIdentity](GojoGo/CoreNetworking/ActingIdentity.swift)** | The one place the app knows who it's publishing as. Read by the stores when they build a create body. **Not persisted** — a relaunch comes back as you, because publishing as the wrong identity is the expensive mistake |
| **Composer banner** | "Posting as \<business\>" with a one-tap way back to yourself, because an identity you switched on an hour ago is exactly the thing you forget |
| **Profile page** | Real verified badge (it was previously drawn on *every* visited profile — decoration, not information), the business block (address / hours / phone / email / site), and, on your own business, "Publish as this" + "Manage" instead of a Follow button you'd be pointing at yourself |

**Deliberately not in this slice:** business profile *deletion* (account deletion is the trust & safety slice, 2e M5), team members / staff roles (SPECS §15 — single owner until GoJoAdmin needs roles), a `delivery.merchant` ↔ business-profile link (the storefront contract, 2e M4), and business discovery beyond a handle (search is Phase 5 M4). `maxBusinessesPerOwner` is a constant (5) until the platform config registry lands in 2e M3.

**Publishing as a business is not the same as owning what you published.** The first E2E run failed exactly one assertion — A deleting the post A had just published as their business — because every ownership check in `social` and `watch` compared the content's author id to the caller. So `ProfileApi` gained a second verb, `actsFor(caller, authorId)`: true for your own content *and* for any business you own, whatever the client is switched to at the time. It now guards post delete, story-frame delete, story viewers + reply delete, highlights (create-from-your-frames / edit / delete), video edit + delete, and video-comment delete. The author-scoped story surfaces that write into an identity's *own* space take an optional `asProfileId` / `actAsProfileId` for the same reason — a business's archive is its own, and so is its close-friends audience.

**Verified in prod (2026-07-30, `verify_business_profiles.sh`, session-local, 24/24):** create → `verified:false` on creation (the badge is the review's) → owner-only reads (`/mine` lists it, B gets 403) → `/v1/me/roles` `hasBusiness` true for A, false for B → the business page reads as an ordinary profile for a stranger (`kind:"BUSINESS"`, business block, `isOwner:false`; true for A) → A posts as the business (author is the business, flagged `business:true`) → **B posting as A's business is 403** → B follows the business and its post lands in B's feed → owner edit 200, non-owner edit 403 → a person still reads `kind:"PERSON"` → the owner deletes the business's post (the check that failed the first run). Migration `V20` applied cleanly on the live database.

**Simulator pass on the live backend (2026-07-30, driven end to end):** own profile → the handle chevron opens **Business profiles** (both businesses loaded from prod, "You" selected) → switch to one → the profile shows the "Publishing as …" chip → composer → **post published as @cheztest2915**, rendered in the feed with the business as author → its `…` menu offers **Delete post** → deleting it sticks (the optimistic removal reverts on refusal, so a post that stays gone is a server 204 — the `actsFor` fix, from the app) → relaunch comes back as **you**, as designed → switching again shows the composer's "Posting as …" banner and **Post as you** clears it.

That pass found two things the curl E2E structurally could not, both fixed in the same session:

| Found | Why it happened | Fix |
|---|---|---|
| The "Posting as…" banner never appeared | It was added to `ComposePostView`, which nothing presents — the composer the app actually opens is `ComposeChrome` in the tab bar | Banner moved to [GGTabBar](GojoGo/Navigation/GGTabBar.swift)'s compose stack (`ComposePostView` is dead code, flagged separately) |
| Your own business's post showed a **Follow** chip, and its menu had no **Delete post** | Both come from "is this mine": `SocialStore.map` compared the author id to `myProfileId`, and `AppState.isOwnChannel` compared the handle to the user's — neither knew about businesses | `SocialStore.ownedBusinessIds` (kept in sync on refresh) and `isOwnChannel` now including owned business handles — the client mirror of the server's `actsFor`. `refreshBusinessProfiles()` also moved **before** `refreshSocial()` in `connectBackend`, or the first screenful renders before the app knows which authors are you |

**Test data left in prod (2e M4):** the two test restaurants carry storefronts — user B's live one has a real six-block page (kept deliberately: it is the only rendering of this feature anywhere, and the restaurant is a test kitchen), and user A's suspended one has a `Storefront E2E` section, two dishes and a `SFRONT10` promotion left by the E2E, with its own page cleared.

**Test data left in prod:** two business profiles named `cheztest…` owned by test user A (their posts were deleted). There is no delete-a-business endpoint by design — account and profile deletion is the trust & safety slice (2e M5) — so they stay until it exists.

## Username change — 2-month cooldown with grace (deployed + verified 2026-07-23)

Users can change their `@handle` from **Edit profile → Username**. Policy: the **first two sets are free** (the onboarding pick + one grace change — e.g. to fix a typo), after which a change is allowed **once every 2 calendar months**. Enforced entirely server-side in the `profile` module (Flyway `V7` adds `handle_changed_at` + `handle_change_count` to `profile.user_profile`; no new AWS infra).

- **One central guard** (`ProfileService.applyHandleChange`) runs for **both** `PATCH /v1/profiles/me` and the dedicated `PUT /v1/profiles/me/handle` — so the cooldown can't be bypassed via PATCH. Rules: no-op if unchanged (doesn't consume a free set); free while `handle_change_count < 2`; after that requires `now ≥ handle_changed_at + 2 months`; target must be valid + un-taken. `409` taken / `429` cooldown (message carries the next-eligible date) / `400` too short. The auto-generated signup handle is not a user change (count starts 0).
- **Availability + status endpoints**: `handle-available` (format + case-insensitive not-taken-by-another, `reason` ok|taken|invalid|current) and `handle-status` (`canChangeNow` / `changeAvailableAt`) drive the iOS UI gate.
- **iOS** — [ChangeUsernameSheet](GojoGo/Screens/ChangeUsernameSheet.swift): debounced live availability (green available / red taken / neutral "current"), a cooldown banner + disabled field when gated, "Save username" → `AppState.changeUsername` (`PUT`, surfaces the backend 429/409 message). Wired into [EditProfileSheet](GojoGo/Screens/ActivityView.swift) (replaced the old "handle can't be changed in the prototype" placeholder). `ProfileStore.handleStatus/checkHandle/changeHandle` + DTOs added.

**Verified (2026-07-23)** — non-destructive prod E2E on user A (`verify_handle.sh`, session-local): availability check returns current/taken/invalid/ok correctly; change to a taken handle → **409**; free set #1 (change away) → 200, still `canChangeNow=true`; grace set #2 (change back, restores A) → 200, then `canChangeNow=false` with `changeAvailableAt` = +2 months; 3rd change → **429** (read-only reject, A unchanged); no-op PUT of the current handle → 200 (not rate-limited); A's handle restored to `gojogom1test` at the end (A's `handle_change_count` is now 2, so A is gated until 2026-09-23 — a real test-data side effect, harmless to other flows).

## Phase 2f · Milestone 1 — Name, lookup and private renames (built + deployed 2026-08-02)

The three pieces of the GojoMessages re-spec that need no phone graph, and therefore aren't waiting on SNS SMS (#3). **No new AWS infra and no migration** — `messaging` is DynamoDB-only, which is what let this be built in the same working tree as Phase 4 M1's `V35` without the two touching.

**1. The product rename.** "My World" → **GojoMessages** in every user-visible string: the mode switcher, the settings screen, the setup flow, the OTP SMS body, the delete-conversation copy. The wire (`/v1/world/**`), the DynamoDB prefixes (`WORLDUSER#` / `WORLDPHONE#`) and the iOS type names (`WorldSocket`, `MyWorldView`, `WorldPreference`) are **deliberately unchanged** — renaming a live URL costs a client release and buys nothing.

**2. Add-by-number only.** `startLiveConversation(handle:)` is deleted, `addWorldContact` no longer resolves a non-numeric entry against the backend, and `startLiveGroup` skips anything that isn't a phone number. `ProfileStore.view(handle:)` stays — a handle is still how a *public* profile is opened, just no longer a way to start a private thread. This is the one built behaviour the re-spec contradicted outright.

**3. Private per-viewer renames.** Rename any contact to whatever you like; you see it everywhere their name appears; they never see it and neither does anyone else. Stored as an `ALIAS#{contactId}` item under the viewer's **own** `WORLDUSER#` partition — an alias is a property of the edge from you to them, so it lives under your key and is unreachable from their reads by construction rather than by a filter. `GET /v1/world/aliases` + `PUT /v1/world/aliases/{contactId}` (blank clears). Account-level rather than device-local: it went next to the contacts graph, not into `WorldPreference` with the mutes and wallpapers, because a rename is about the person and not about this handset.

**The design call worth recording — the alias is a client-side lens, not a server rewrite.** The plan in ARCHITECTURE said resolve it per-caller in `toConversationDto` / `toMessageDto`. That is wrong, and building it is what showed why: **the WebSocket fan-out publishes one `MessageDto` to every participant at once**, so a name resolved server-side would be right over REST and wrong over the socket — precisely the inconsistency a rename is supposed to eliminate. So the wire keeps carrying real names and ids, and the lens sits in `MessagingStore.map`, where DTOs become app models; every surface that renders a name inherits it without knowing it exists.

Two consequences of that:

- **Push is the single exception.** A device can't rewrite an APNs alert after delivery, so a push is the one name the server renders on a specific viewer's behalf. `MessageSent` gained `senderNameByRecipient` (+ a `titleFor(recipientId)` accessor) and `notifyRecipients` fills it with a targeted alias lookup per recipient; `MessageNotificationListener` resolves the title inside its existing loop. `notifications` never learns what an alias is.
- **Quoted replies needed an id.** `buildReply` stores `authorName` **into the message row**, one string every viewer reads. Rather than stop storing it, `ReplySnippetDto` gained **`authorId`**; the client aliases the quote by id and the stored name remains the fallback for snippets written before the field existed (Jackson decodes the absent field as null, and iOS decodes it optionally). Without this the rename would have been right in the bubble and wrong in the quote directly above it.

**Verified:** backend compiles and `ModularityTests` is green on Corretto 21 — `messaging` gained no dependency on a vertical, and `notifications → messaging` still goes through the published event. iOS builds clean for the simulator. **Not verified:** nothing here has run against the deployed backend or in a simulator with two identities — the alias round-trip, the rename showing on the conversation list, the push title, and the reply-quote fallback are all still unexercised. **Not built:** circles server-side, GojoMessages content (posts/stories/carousels), the rich profile, and the feed home screen — the half of Phase 2f that needs the phone graph, and therefore SNS SMS.

## Phase 2f · Milestone 2 — The private network itself (built + deployed 2026-08-02)

Everything the re-spec asks for on top of M1: a real graph, circles that are an audience rather than a folder, GojoMessages' own content, the rich profile, and the feed home screen. **No new AWS infra and no migration** — all DynamoDB in the existing messaging table, which is what kept the whole phase clear of Phase 4 M1's `V35`.

**The four open questions are now closed.** In order: content **extends `messaging`** rather than getting its own module; storage **stays DynamoDB**; the two networks **do not share stories**; and the schools database is **free text with no vendor**. The reasoning for each is in ARCHITECTURE §Phase 2f, struck through in place.

**The graph.** A contact is two items written in one transaction — the edge (`WORLDUSER#{owner}` → `CONTACT#{contact}`) and its reverse (`CONTACTOF#{contact}` → `OWNER#{owner}`). The reverse edge is not redundancy; it answers *"who holds my number"*, which is what decides whether somebody may be shown a number and added back. **It is no longer the audience.** Until 2026-08-02 a post went to everyone who had added the author, which meant anybody with your number could put themselves in your audience and you could not put them out again; the audience is now the author's own contact list, so adding somebody is the act of letting them in and dropping them is the act of shutting them out. Reaching somebody by number used to add them outright, on the theory that starting a thread and joining somebody's audience are the same act. **Corrected 2026-08-02:** they are two acts, and only one of them is a message. Opening a thread and answering one now both leave the graph alone, and each side gets an **Add** bar in the thread (and a card on the contact page) to make the other decision when they mean to. Two things were wrong with the old behaviour and only one was consent: because content is fanned out on write, an add that delivered no history left the person you'd just added reading as somebody who had never posted — so an add now hands over what they published to their contacts already, and dropping them takes it back. The number an add is made of is disclosed by `GET /v1/world/profile/{id}` to a contact **and** to anyone that person reached first (they hold you as a contact, or you share a 1:1), which is what makes adding back possible without ever weakening *a number is the only way in* — a stranger's number is never returned, so a stranger can never be added.

**Circles are an audience now.** Server-side, owned by the person who made them, and a circle **refuses any member who isn't already a contact** — refused rather than silently filtered, so a caller can never believe it published to somebody it didn't. Dropping a contact removes them from every circle in the same call, because a stale circle is a stale audience.

**Content, and the one property worth defending.** Posts, stories (24h TTL) and carousels, published to `CONTACTS` or to named `CIRCLES`. `WorldAudience` has exactly two constants and **no `PUBLIC`**, with a test that fails the build if a third ever appears — an executable version of the rule that a GojoMessages post can never become public.

The safety property is **structural rather than diligent**: content is **fanned out on write** to exactly the resolved audience, and a reader only ever queries their own feed partition. There is no audience filter on the read path, so there is none to forget. Three consequences follow, all deliberate:

- **"What has this person published?"** (the contact page tab) is answered from the **viewer's** partition filtered by author, not from the author's partition filtered by audience. Both give the same answer on a good day; only one of them can leak on a bad one.
- **The audience is stripped from every reader's copy.** The author sees which circles they chose; a reader is never told which of them they landed in.
- **The cost is writes proportional to audience size.** For a network whose premise is the few hundred people you actually know, that is the right trade against a read-time filter that has to be correct in every query anybody ever adds.

**The rich profile** (bio, gender, city, languages, education, occupation, interests, favourite books/films/TV/sport/games) hangs off the **phone-verified identity**, stored as one JSON attribute on the existing profile item so reading an identity stays a single get. It comes back **only to an actual contact** — knowing a number is how you reach someone, not a licence to read where they went to school.

**iOS.** The home screen is a feed: stories row, then posts, then the messages list, with the chat detail screen **untouched** — the re-spec says so explicitly and it is the one finished part of this product. Plus a composer with the audience picker, a story viewer, a circles manager, the profile editor, and the contact page's new Posts tab. "Add by Username" is gone from the menu. All the new modals ride the existing `WorldSheetKind` binding rather than getting booleans of their own, since `WorldSheetHost` is the single owner and a second presenter attached to a live screen silently swallows the first.

**Six defects found and fixed in a review pass the same day**, four of them real bugs rather than polish:

1. **Publishing looked like it did nothing.** Fan-out deliberately skips the author (writing somebody a copy of their own post is pure duplication) — but the one feed the author reads was then the one feed they were never written into, so a post or story vanished on publish. `feed()` now merges the caller's own copies in from their author partition, which also means their own posts come back carrying the audience they chose.
2. **Delete could leave copies behind.** It re-resolved the audience at delete time, so anybody dropped from a contact list or edited out of a circle *after* publishing kept the post forever, in a feed the author could no longer reach. A post now records the viewer list it was fanned out to, and delete works from that.
3. **The contact page hid older posts behind a busy feed.** `byAuthorForViewer` took the first 100 items of the viewer's feed and *then* filtered by author, so an active feed made a quiet contact look like they had never posted. It now pages the whole partition and filters as it goes — still the viewer's own partition, so it still cannot return anything they weren't sent.
4. **`carousel` was a kind the server accepted and nothing ever sent.** A post with more than one image is now published as a carousel, which is what the re-spec means by the third kind.
5. **The composer always opened on Post**, including from the bubble labelled "Your story". It now takes a starting kind from whoever opens it.
6. **"Create a circle" from inside the composer reassigned the sheet binding that was presenting it** — the reliable way to dismiss a sheet and present nothing. Circle creation is now a nested sheet owned by the composer.

Also added in the same pass: **the `+` button in the GojoMessages top bar** (the primary way into publishing — the story bubble is secondary), **your own story ring** in the stories row with a `+` badge for a second frame, and a **retry** on the contact page instead of a permanently empty tab after one failed request.

**Verified:** the full backend suite is green on Corretto 21 — **320 tests plus 6 new ones** covering the audience model, the reader-copy strip, alias precedence and per-recipient push titles. `ModularityTests` green: `messaging` still exposes only `MessagingApi`, so no vertical can reach the content. iOS builds clean for the simulator. **Not verified:** none of Phase 2f has run against the deployed backend or in a simulator with two identities. Nothing in the graph, the fan-out, the feed, the circles or the profile has been exercised once. **Blocked:** real phone verification is still SNS-sandboxed (#3), so in production only verified test numbers can join a graph at all — this phase ships to a locked door until that clears.

## Incidents & fixes log

- **"Couldn't take the stake" — while the $30 had already moved (2026-08-02, reported from a real signup; the same DTO-date defect this log recorded on 2026-07-27, reintroduced by Phase 3 M2's own DTO):** a driver tapping **Lock $30.00 stake** got a red *Couldn't take the stake.* banner, every time, forever. **The backend was never involved in the failure.** `POST /v1/partner/applications/{id}/stake` moved the money, wrote the ledger entry and returned `200` — and the app then failed to *decode its own success*. `PartnerStakeDTO.paidAt` was typed `Date`, and `APIClient`'s shared decoder has no `dateDecodingStrategy`, so `.deferredToDate` wanted a number and got the backend's ISO-8601 string. The timing is what made it invisible: `paidAt` is **null right up until the stake is paid**, so the stake page loaded perfectly and only the response reporting success ever threw. The thrown `DecodingError` isn't an `APIError.http`, so `message(from:fallback:)` fell through to the fallback string — a decode bug wearing a payment error's clothes. Two things then welded the dead end shut: the same `paidAt` broke every subsequent `GET /v1/partner/me`, and `refreshDriverApplication` was a `try?` that **assigned `nil` on failure**, so the page dropped back to its hardcoded `?? 3000` and a shortfall of zero — which is why the screenshot showed a plausible $30 / $100 / *Due today* while the server held a live stake and a `$70` balance. Fixed on all four levels: every DTO timestamp is a `String` parsed with `BackendDate` (the convention, now also applied to `dispatch`, `travel`, `kyc`, `delivery` and the safety payloads — `DispatchOfferDTO.expiresAt` and `RideOfferDTO.expiresAt` were non-optional, so **no offer or counteroffer had ever decoded**); the shared decoder is taught the backend's format anyway, so a `Date` added later just works; the refresh keeps its last good application instead of blanking it; and the stake's error path now **re-reads the application before it accuses the server** — the movement is idempotency-keyed, so "the request failed" and "the money didn't move" are different statements, and anyone already stranded is carried straight to the ID check on their next tap. **Lesson, twice now: a timestamp that is null until the operation succeeds turns a decode bug into a failure that fires only on success — the most expensive place a schema mistake can hide. A convention documented in this log is not a guard; the decoder is.**

- **A ride confirmed inside a transaction that had already committed (2026-08-01, found by the first prod E2E of Phase 3, defect shipped in M3):** a driver accepting a dispatch offer at the asking price got a 500 — `TransactionRequiredException: no transaction is in progress` — from `rides.saveAndFlush` inside `RideService.confirmFromDispatch`. The method is annotated `@Transactional` and it *was* called through the proxy; the trap is that it runs from an **`AFTER_COMMIT` listener**, where the just-committed transaction's resources are still bound to the thread, so `REQUIRED` joins a transaction that is finished rather than starting a fresh one. The visible damage was worse than the 500: by the time the listener runs, **dispatch has already assigned the job**, so the driver was flipped to BUSY for a ride that never left `REQUESTED`, and the rider saw nothing at all until the 5-minute request TTL expired and the sweep released the worker. (That sweep is the only reason this was self-healing rather than a wedged driver.) Fixed with `propagation = REQUIRES_NEW` on **both** listener entry points — `confirmFromDispatch` and `exhaust`, which writes too — plus [`ListenerTransactionTests`](backend/src/test/java/com/gojogo/travel/internal/ListenerTransactionTests.java), which asserts the rule by reflection rather than behaviour, because reproducing it needs a real transaction manager, a real commit and a real publisher and this machine has none of those. **Why it survived M3's deploy:** the *negotiated* path confirms inside the rider's own request transaction and works perfectly, and it is the path every test and every manual check used. The broken one needs an approved driver accepting at the asking price, and there was no approved driver in existence until today. **Lesson, and it is the same one this file keeps writing down in different words: a path nothing has ever executed is not a working path.** 212 green unit tests, a clean `ModularityTests`, a successful deploy and a 200 health check all said nothing about it — the only thing that could ever have found it was somebody actually taking a ride.

- **The region the guard needed was a defect in the app, not a gap in the job (2026-08-01, follow-up to the entry below):** `AWS_REGION: us-east-1` made `schema-check` green, but it made it green by giving the application something the workflow's own comment promises it will not have — and the comment is the more valuable of the two, because "boot with every vendor unconfigured" is a standing test that unconfigured really is a mode. `messaging` failed that test, and so did `media`: `DynamoDbClient.create()`, `S3Presigner.create()` and `S3Client.create()` all ran at **bean construction**, and the SDK resolves a region eagerly there, so an environment without one could not start at all. (`kyc`, `payments`, APNs and `Fanout` were never counter-examples — they build nothing until the first call.) Both modules now do the same: the client is built on the first call that needs it, the module logs at startup that it is **off** (`MESSAGING_TABLE` / `MEDIA_BUCKET` are the switches), a call made anyway answers **503**, and the send-later poller skips its pass instead of logging a 503 twice a minute. `DynamoConfig` no longer configures Dynamo, so it is `MessagingConfig` — kept because its `@EnableScheduling` is the only one in the application and switches on *every* module's `@Scheduled` methods. `AWS_REGION` came back out of the workflow, and the job passes without it: **31 migrations applied to an empty Postgres, every mapping validated, both modules logging themselves off** (run `30683208302`). **Lesson:** when a CI job needs a variable added to go green, ask whether the job was wrong or whether it just found something — this one had found a module that turned "this feature isn't configured here" into "the application does not start", which in a different environment is an outage rather than a red check.

- **The guard added after the four-deploy milestone had never once run (found 2026-08-01, during the "Needs YOU" re-audit):** `schema-check` — the workflow added on 2026-07-31 precisely because two migration defects had reached production — had **failed on all three runs it had ever had, including the run that introduced it**, and nobody had looked, because the deploy workflow beside it was green every time and that is the one that says whether prod moved. So for two days the only thing standing between a migration and production was a job that had never validated a migration. The cause was not the schema and not a migration: the AWS SDK resolves its region **eagerly, at bean construction**, so `messaging`'s `DynamoDbClient.create()` threw during context startup and the run died long before Hibernate's `ddl-auto: validate` had anything to say. Every failure looked like a boot failure because it *was* one — just not the kind the job exists to catch, which is why the red never read as informative. One line fixes it (`AWS_REGION: us-east-1` in the job env; credentials stay unset on purpose, since the client is constructed and never called), and the next run passed with all 31 migrations applied to an empty Postgres and every mapping validated. **Lesson: a CI job that has never been green is not a guard, it is a decoration** — a check whose first run fails and whose failure blocks nothing decays exactly like the "NOT deployed" labels above, and for the same reason. Worth a second look: the workflow's own comment claims every external vendor "treats unconfigured as a mode rather than a failure", and `DynamoConfig` is a standing counter-example to that claim — it needs a region even when it will never make a call. *(Followed up the same day — see the entry above; the region line is gone and the modules were fixed instead.)*

- **Four deploys to ship one milestone, and three of them were things nothing local could catch (2026-07-31, Phase 2e M3):** the first task crash-looped on `BeanDefinitionOverrideException` — payments named its webhook-dedupe entity `ProviderEvent`, matching its column names, and so does `kyc`. Two classes with the same simple name in different packages collide **twice**: on the Spring bean name their repositories get, and on Hibernate's entity name. (This is the same failure mode as the 2026-07-23 `CurrentProfile` collision, which is why every module's profile helper is uniquely named — the lesson had been learned for `@Component`s and not for entities.) Renamed to `StripeEvent`; the table is still `payments.provider_event`, because the collision is Java-side. A sweep for duplicate entity and bean names across all modules comes back clean. The second failed on **`ALTER TABLE … CONSTRAINT`** — inside `ALTER TABLE` every clause is its own `ADD`, and the inline form that reads fine in a `CREATE TABLE` is a syntax error. The third failed Hibernate schema validation: `CHAR(3)` reports as `bpchar` and a plain `String` field expects `varchar`, so `currency` refused to start — fixed forward in `V25`, since editing an applied migration changes its checksum, and `VARCHAR(3)` is what every other currency column in this database already was. **The deploy script called the first of those a success**, which is the more interesting half: it waits for `services-stable` and then curls `/actuator/health`, and *both of those questions are answered by whatever is behind the load balancer* — which, after a failed rollout, is the old task. It now asks what is actually running and insists it is the revision the deploy just created. **Root cause of all three:** there is no Postgres and no Docker on the build machine and RDS is private, so `mvn test` proves the code compiles and the modules don't reach into each other, and says **nothing** about whether the application can start. New [`.github/workflows/schema-check.yml`](.github/workflows/schema-check.yml) closes that: a Postgres service, every migration from `V1` on an empty database, and a boot with `ddl-auto: validate` — with every external vendor left unconfigured, so it doubles as a standing test that "unconfigured is a mode, not a failure" is still true. **Lesson:** a green test suite that never starts the Spring context is not evidence that the app starts; and a deploy check that only asks the load balancer cannot tell "it worked" from "it didn't change anything".

- **Every KYC document link was unopenable (found 2026-07-30 by the 2e M2 E2E; live since 2b M6 shipped):** `GET …/admin/applications/{id}` returned correctly-signed S3 GET URLs that all answered **403 AccessDenied**. A presigned URL carries the *signer's* permissions, and the ECS task role had `grantPut` + `grantDelete` on the media bucket but no `s3:GetObject` — the app never reads media back, because public `media/*` is world-readable and the client always fetches by plain URL. The private prefix is the one place that isn't true, and the reviewer surface is its only consumer, so nothing ever exercised it: M6's verification checked that a link was *returned*, not that it *worked*. **Fixed** with a prefix-scoped `props.mediaBucket.grantRead(taskRole, 'private/*')` in [fargate-stack.ts](infra/lib/fargate-stack.ts) (deployed pinned to the running image tag so the rollout couldn't knock the service back onto `latest`), and re-verified against prod: the link now returns the object. **Lesson:** a presigned URL is only as good as the role that signed it — and "the endpoint returns a URL" is not a test, following it is.

- **You could publish as a business but not delete it (found 2026-07-30 by the 2e M1 E2E's first run, before sign-off):** `POST /v1/posts` with `actAsProfileId` authored the post as the business, and `DELETE /v1/posts/{id}` then returned **403** to the very person who wrote it — `PostService.delete` compared `post.authorId` to the caller's profile id, and the author was the business. The same shape sat in seven other checks (story delete, story viewers, story reply delete, highlights ×2, video edit/delete, video comment delete): every one of them was written when "author" and "caller" could only ever be the same profile. **Fixed** by making that assumption explicit as a public verb — `ProfileApi.actsFor(caller, profileId)` (your own, or a business you own) — and routing all eight through it, plus an optional `asProfileId` on the author-scoped story reads that write into an identity's own space (archive, highlights, close friends). **Lesson:** adding a second identity a user can author as invalidates every `authorId.equals(me)` in the codebase, not just the create paths — and only an E2E that *creates then manages* content catches it, because create and delete were tested separately everywhere else.

- **Seller stats 500'd in prod once real rows existed (found 2026-07-30, by the prod curl E2E M5 still owed; shipped broken since the M5 build):** `GET /v1/economy/listings/mine/stats` returned 500 — `failed to access class com.gojogo.economy.internal.ListingStatus from class jdk.proxy2.$Proxy…`. `ListingRepository.SellerStatusTotals` is a Spring Data **interface projection**, which Spring materializes as a JDK dynamic proxy, and its `getStatus()` returns the **package-private** enum `ListingStatus` — the proxy class lives in another runtime package, so the access check fails *at row-materialization time*. With zero rows the query returns an empty list and nothing materializes, which is why the endpoint 200'd on an empty shelf, passed the simulator check (pre-V10 backend → 404 → optimistic UI), and slipped through: no local test ever hit stats with data. **Fixed** by making `ListingStatus` `public` (still in `internal`, so Modulith boundaries are untouched — Java visibility and module boundaries are enforced separately). Re-verified on prod post-roll: create → pause → edit → sold → stats correct → delete. **Lesson:** an interface projection's getters must return public types; and an endpoint that 200s empty has not been tested until it has rows.

- **Deploy audit (2026-07-30):** the milestone list said 2b M5, 2b M6 and 2c were "built, NOT deployed" — in fact **CI had deployed all of them**: a push to `main` with backend changes auto-deploys, ECS was running image `3082344` (= HEAD), and Flyway had applied through `V19`. Live probes confirmed M5/M6/2c endpoints 200, `PARTNER_ADMIN_TOKEN` wired from Secrets Manager (admin list 200 with the token, 404 without — as designed), delivery catalog empty-but-live. Statuses above corrected. **Lesson:** with CI auto-deploy on `main`, "not deployed" labels go stale the moment work is pushed — verify against the running image tag, not the doc.

- **Every 404/409 on a token-guarded path was really a bodyless 401 (found 2026-07-27 while building the partner review surface; pre-existing since the economy cleanup endpoints shipped):** Spring renders a `ResponseStatusException` by *forwarding to `/error`*, and Spring Security 6 authorizes that ERROR dispatch as well. `/error` wasn't permitted, so it fell to `anyRequest().authenticated()` and a caller with no JWT — which is the entire point of these endpoints — got `401` with an empty body instead of the documented `404 {"message": …}`. Authenticated endpoints were never affected (the caller passes the check, which is why every previous milestone's error messages worked). **Fixed** by permitting `/error` in [SecurityConfig](backend/src/main/java/com/gojogo/SecurityConfig.java); it exposes nothing of its own, only the status and message of the request that just failed. **Lesson:** a `permitAll` path isn't fully permitted until its error dispatch is too — and "documented behaviour" nobody exercised with a real curl is a guess.

- **iOS DTO dates must be `String`, not `Date` (2026-07-27, partner milestone):** `APIClient`'s shared `JSONDecoder` has no `dateDecodingStrategy`, and the backend emits nanosecond fractions, so a `Date` field fails to decode. The store caught the error and logged it in DEBUG only, so the app simply behaved as if the user had no partner account while the endpoint was answering perfectly — the most expensive kind of failure to chase. The codebase convention (from Milestone 4) is a raw `String` parsed with `BackendDate.parse` at mapping time; every other store already did this. **Lesson:** when a store swallows its own decode errors, a schema mistake looks like an empty state.

- **A left 1:1 could never be reopened (2026-07-24, found by the M4 E2E, pre-existing since Phase 2 M1):** `DELETE /v1/conversations/{id}` deletes the caller's membership row, but nothing rewrites the `DIRECT#{a}#{b}` dedupe pointer. The next `POST /v1/conversations` for that pair resolved the pointer to the dead thread and then `orElseThrow`'d on the missing membership → **500 "Membership missing"**, permanently, for that pair. A quieter second half: the per-send membership bump is a DynamoDB `Update`, which *recreates* a deleted membership item — but only with the attributes in the expression, so the resurrected row had no `convId` (unreadable: NPE in `readMembership`) and no `gsi1pk` (invisible to the `gsi1` list query). Messages sent to someone who had left went nowhere. **Fixed** in [MessagingService.createConversation](backend/src/main/java/com/gojogo/messaging/internal/MessagingService.java) (reuse the pointer only when the conversation still exists *and* still lists the caller; otherwise create fresh and overwrite it; a caller with no membership row is rejoined via `repo.rejoin`) and [MessagingRepository.appendMessage](backend/src/main/java/com/gojogo/messaging/internal/MessagingRepository.java) (the bump re-states `convId` + `gsi1pk` every time). Re-verified: both leave → re-create returns the thread → A's list has it → A sends → **B's list gets the row back** with the new preview. **Lesson:** in a single-table design a dedupe pointer is a second source of truth — validate what it resolves to; and a DynamoDB `Update` is an upsert, so any expression that can run against a deleted item must write enough to leave a *valid, index-visible* item.

- **Economy save-count double-bump (2026-07-23, caught in the first E2E, fixed before sign-off):** `ListingService.save` used `saveAndFlush(new SavedListing(...))` in a `try/catch (DataIntegrityViolationException)` to be idempotent, then `bumpSaveCount(+1)`. But `SavedListing` (like `PostLike`/`PostBookmark`/`CommentLike`) uses an `@IdClass` with **assigned** (non-generated) ids, so Spring Data `save()` runs a JPA **merge** (select-then-update), *not* a persist/insert — a duplicate save never throws, silently UPDATEs the row, and the count bumps again (E2E showed `save_count`=2 after two saves). **Fixed** by guarding on `saves.existsById(new SavedListing.Key(...))` before the save+bump (try/catch kept only as a concurrent-insert backstop). Re-verified 3× save → count 1. **Lesson:** for assigned-id join entities, don't rely on a duplicate-key exception for idempotency — check existence first. The **same latent bug exists in `social`** (`PostService.like`/`bookmark`, `CommentService.like`) — flagged as a separate task (pre-existing; the double-like count case was never exercised).

- **Notifications deploy (2026-07-23):** first roll **auto-rolled-back** — startup `ConflictingBeanDefinitionException`: both `messaging.internal.CurrentProfile` and `notifications.internal.CurrentProfile` took the default bean name `currentProfile`. Neither `mvn compile` nor the `ModularityTests` boot the full Spring context, so it only surfaced at runtime; App Runner's health check caught it and rolled back to the prior image (no downtime — `/v1/world/*` kept serving). Fixed by renaming to `NotificationCurrentProfile`. **Lesson:** two `@Component`s with the same simple class name across modules collide; keep bean class names unique (or set an explicit `@Component("name")`).

- **Social sign-in deploy (2026-07-23):** first `cdk deploy GojoGoAuthStack` failed with a **circular dependency** — the pool referenced the trigger Lambda (`lambdaTriggers`) while `userPool.grant(lambda, …)` put the pool's generated ARN in the Lambda's role policy. Fixed by scoping that grant to a static `arn:aws:cognito-idp:<region>:<account>:userpool/*` ([auth-stack.ts](infra/lib/auth-stack.ts)) instead of `userPool.userPoolArn` (the PreSignUp Lambda reads the real pool id from the trigger event anyway). Redeployed clean. The app-client updated in place (id preserved). App Runner service update took ~4.5 min.

- **M3 session:** CloudFront distribution creation is blocked — **the AWS account is unverified for CloudFront** (new-account restriction; only AWS Support can lift it). Interim: media served public-read directly from S3. When support verifies the account, flip `ENABLE_CLOUDFRONT = true` in [media-stack.ts](infra/lib/media-stack.ts) and redeploy `GojoGoMediaStack` + `GojoGoAppStack` — URLs keep their paths, only the domain changes. Also: a CDK env-var update to the App Runner service did **not** re-pull `:latest` — after pushing a new image, always run `aws apprunner start-deployment` even if a CFN update just deployed.

- **M2 session:** an interrupted `cdk deploy` had left `GojoGoAppStack` as a `REVIEW_IN_PROGRESS` shell with the App Runner service deleted — fixed by deleting the stack shell and redeploying (service URL changed as a result). Spring Data derived `deleteBy…` methods on `@IdClass` entities threw `ClassCastException` in prod — replaced with explicit `@Modifying @Query` deletes ([Repositories.java](backend/src/main/java/com/gojogo/social/internal/Repositories.java)).
- **Private networking attempt (user, reverted):** App Runner VPC egress routes *all* outbound traffic through the VPC, so an isolated VPC broke Cognito JWT validation. Real fix needs a NAT Gateway (~$32–35/mo) — deferred to the ECS/Fargate migration.

## Known issues / dev shortcuts to revisit

- ~~**RDS publicly accessible**~~ — **done, and this entry was stale.** Verified against the account 2026-07-31: the live instance is `postgresv4`, `PubliclyAccessible: false`, in private subnets, with NAT `nat-00f4d90a5aeeb3e82` running. Both phases of [infra/PRIVATE_RDS_MIGRATION.md](infra/PRIVATE_RDS_MIGRATION.md) were applied at some point after 2026-07-25 without this file being updated.
- ~~**GitHub Actions workflow untested**~~ — **stale.** CI has been deploying since 2026-07-25 ("Needs YOU" #4), and the manual command this entry gave was an App Runner one, which no longer exists. Deploy with `./scripts/deploy-backend.sh` (code) or `--infra` (the stack); CI runs the same script, so the two cannot drift.
- **Signup requires `admin-confirm-sign-up`** or emailed code — decide UX before launch.
- Feed `following` decoration loads the full followee id set per request — **now loaded once** per feed request (was twice: `feed` + `decorate`; deduped 2026-07-24). Still an in-memory set per request; cache/join if the followee count ever gets large.
- App Runner bills ~24/7 (~$25/mo with RDS); `aws apprunner pause-service` when idle.
- **Account linking is one-directional** (email → federated). The Lambda links a *new* Google/Apple sign-in to an *existing* email user. The reverse — someone who used **Google first** and later tries to **self-sign-up with email/password** on the same address — still hits the email-alias uniqueness and fails at sign-up (they should keep using Google). **Deployed 2026-07-23 (`GojoGoAuthStack`), pending behavioral E2E:** a `PreSignUp_SignUp` handler in [auth-triggers/index.mjs](infra/lambda/auth-triggers/index.mjs) now detects the pre-existing federated user and returns a clear "continue with Google/Apple" message instead of an opaque Cognito failure (it can't silently merge — a native password can't be attached to a federation-only user from a trigger). **To test:** Google-first-then-email-signup on device; confirm the message renders in `CognitoAuthClient`. Still open by design: if Apple withholds the email (private-relay off), that Apple identity gets a synthetic `@appleid.gojogo` username and won't link to a real-email account.
- **Media is served straight from S3** (public-read on `media/*`) until AWS Support verifies the account for CloudFront — see incidents log. **Orphan cleanup — deployed 2026-07-23 (`GojoGoAppStack` + backend image; V5 migration applied, health UP), running report-only:** presigned keys are now tracked in `media.upload_object` ([V5 migration](backend/src/main/resources/db/migration/V5__media_uploads.sql)); modules call `MediaApi.markReferenced` when they persist a URL (posts, stories, message attachments, social + World avatars); [MediaCleanupJob](backend/src/main/java/com/gojogo/media/internal/MediaCleanupJob.java) sweeps daily at 03:30 UTC. Live env `MEDIA_CLEANUP_DELETE=false` → it logs the orphans it *would* delete and removes nothing. **To enable deletion:** after the daily sweep, check App Runner logs for `Media orphan sweep (report-only)` and confirm no in-use media is flagged, then set `MEDIA_CLEANUP_DELETE=true`. Note: pre-V5 uploads aren't tracked, so they're never flagged or deleted (conservative).
- **Terminology (2026-08-02):** everything below written as **"My World"** is now the product called **GojoMessages**. The wire (`/v1/world/**`), the DynamoDB prefixes (`WORLDUSER#` / `WORLDPHONE#`) and the iOS type names still say World, deliberately — renaming a live URL costs a client release and buys nothing, so the split is product name now, identifiers whenever a file is being edited anyway. See ARCHITECTURE §2b decision 4.
- **My World OTP has a dev bypass code** `WORLD_OTP_DEV_CODE=424242` (App Runner env, set in [app-stack.ts](infra/lib/app-stack.ts)) that verifies any number without a real SMS — because SNS SMS is almost certainly still in the account's **sandbox** (only verified destination numbers, ~$1/mo cap). Real delivery needs SNS SMS production access (AWS Support) + a registered origination/sender id; then **clear `WORLD_OTP_DEV_CODE`** before launch. The World profile is separate from the social profile by design (WhatsApp model).
- **Marketplace test-data cleanup is an opt-in endpoint** — `GET /v1/economy/admin/listings` and `POST /v1/economy/admin/listings/purge` ([EconomyAdminController](backend/src/main/java/com/gojogo/economy/internal/EconomyAdminController.java)) list and delete listings for clearing test data. They sit **outside the JWT chain** (one curl, no user token) and guard themselves with the `X-Economy-Admin-Token` header against `ECONOMY_ADMIN_TOKEN`; with no token — the deployed default, since [fargate-stack.ts](infra/lib/fargate-stack.ts) sets it to `''` unless `-c economyAdminToken=…` is passed — every path under `/v1/economy/admin` 404s. **Enable only for the deploy that does the wipe, then deploy again without the flag** (a plain deploy turns it back off on its own). Purge takes the listing's photos and saves with it via `ON DELETE CASCADE`; the S3 images are left to MediaCleanupJob's orphan sweep.
- **~~Simulator MCP panel blocked~~ (resolved)**: `xcode-select` now points at Xcode 26.2; the app builds via `xcodebuild`. Original note: `xcode-select` doesn't point at Xcode — fix with `sudo xcode-select -s /Applications/Xcode.app/Contents/Developer` (needs user password).
- All milestone work is committed locally; push to GitHub when ready (`git push`).

## To resume in a new session (rewritten 2026-08-02)

**Everything through Phase 4 M1 is deployed — including the two milestones the paragraph below used to call undeployed.** Checked against the live account on 2026-08-02: task definition **rev 45**, image **`e333f24`**, `V35`/`V36`/`V37` applied and prod at schema **v37**, health 200 through the ALB, `origin/main` level with local. So **Phase 4 M1 (real couriers)** and **Phase 2f (GojoMessages M1 + M2)** are both live; CI rolled them off the push exactly as it has every other time. That is the **eighth** decay of a label in this file, and the shape has never varied: *nothing fails when a thing is done and the doc isn't updated*. What is still true of both is the *other* half — **neither has been exercised in production even once** (see the two paragraphs below), and "deployed" and "known to work" have been different claims in this repo since 2026-08-01. *(Check the running image and `git fetch` before believing any of this.)*

**The thing to have known before that deploy went out, kept because it is now history rather than a warning.** `V35` deliberately left in-flight orders alone, because a migration cannot release an escrow hold; `OrderTimeoutJob` cancelled and refunded them on its first passes after startup. Any delivery order still moving when it rolled was therefore cancelled — correct, and by then almost certainly an empty set, since the delivery catalog has been empty (every merchant SUSPENDED) since the 2026-08-01 cleanup.

**Two things that are worth doing before the next milestone rather than after:**

1. **Exercise M5 in prod.** It is deployed and **entirely unexercised** — 293 green unit tests said nothing about M3's transaction defect either. The paths nothing has ever run: the `TripCompleted` → invitation listener (another `AFTER_COMMIT` listener, written with `REQUIRES_NEW` from the start *because* of that incident), the reward transfer out of STAKING, the flag-and-suspend on a "no", the expiry sweep, and the public **`GET /v1/share/{token}`** — the first unauthenticated read this API has ever served that returns a person's live position, so it is worth confirming by hand that an expired token, a revoked one and a made-up one are byte-identical 404s. A full run needs an approved driver with an APPROVED-but-unverified vehicle and a completed trip; the 2026-08-01 E2E section has the account setup.
2. **Decide whether to spend one Sumsub sandbox cycle** proving the vendor verdict path (see "Needs YOU" #13). It is still the biggest unexercised path in the system and every driver and merchant goes through it.

**Phase 4 M2 and M3 turned out to be deployed after all — the ninth decay, and the fastest one yet.** Checked against the live account on 2026-08-04 before starting M4: task definition **rev 55**, image **`85627ca`**, which is `HEAD` — the merge that carried both — and health 200 through the ALB, so `V38` and `V40` are applied. The label saying otherwise was written on 2026-08-04 and was stale within hours, because CI deploys from the remote and the merge went out on its own. The shape has never varied: *nothing fails when a thing is done and the doc isn't.* What is still true of both is the other half — **neither has been exercised in production even once**.

**Phase 4 M4 (pickup / collect-in-store) is what is genuinely not deployed**, built 2026-08-04. It needs **`V41`** applied and an image rolled, and no `cdk deploy` — no new secret, env var or IAM action. `mvn test` 478/478, iOS `BUILD SUCCEEDED`. **The deploy strands nothing**: `V41` is three `ADD COLUMN`s with defaults and one config row, the column default *is* the backfill, and every order that exists stays a DELIVERY. The one thing to know is that collection is **off for every merchant** until an owner turns it on in *Your restaurant* — which is deliberate (it advertises a service somebody has to staff), and which means nothing visibly changes for a customer until a restaurant opts in.

**The plan's next milestone is the rest of Phase 4 M4's breadth, and the order SPECS §5 implies is scheduled orders first** — a scheduled pickup is the same poller with no `pickupLead`, which is exactly why collection was worth doing before it, and a scheduled *delivery* is the same poller with one. Then modify/cancel before preparation, order-for-someone-else, and category expansion (grocery, pharmacy, retail — merchant categories and order kinds on this model, not new modules).
